{"parentUuid":null,"isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":"Research task. You are gathering evidence for a design decision, not writing an essay. The standard is: reputable sources only (peer-reviewed venues, arXiv primaries, official engineering documentation from the companies that built the systems), every claim carries a retrievable citation, and you record EVERY search query you ran so the research is auditable. Anything you cannot source, mark `unverifiable`. No guesswork.\n\nQUESTION: What is actually MEASURED about why multi-agent LLM systems fail, and what coordination protocols measurably reduce those failures?\n\nCover specifically:\n1. The MAST taxonomy (Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy, arXiv 2503.13657 or similar) — the actual failure categories and their measured percentages. Get the real numbers.\n2. Any study measuring multi-agent vs single-agent performance on the same task — where does multi-agent WIN and where does it LOSE? I want the cases where it loses, specifically.\n3. Coordination overhead: is there measured evidence on how cost/latency scales with agent count?\n4. Inter-agent misalignment, specification drift, and information loss between agents — what is measured about how a task spec degrades as it is passed down?\n5. Verification/review stages in multi-agent pipelines — is there measured evidence that an adversarial or independent verifier stage improves final output quality?\n\nReturn a structured report:\n- FINDINGS: each with the number, the source (title, venue, year, URL/arXiv id), and one line on what it implies for designing a divide-and-conquer agent protocol.\n- SEARCHES RUN: the exact query strings, in order.\n- GAPS: what you could NOT find a source for. Be explicit and honest — a named gap is worth more than a plausible sentence.\n\nDo not pad. Numbers and citations only."},"uuid":"f7cc1fb9-4474-49e5-8f0a-48f311445e96","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:22:35.581Z","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

{"parentUuid":"f51a8e25-b2bf-4011-a5c3-7c8fefbb10ff","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","attachment":{"type":"skill_listing","content":"- graphify: Use for any question about a codebase, its architecture, file relationships, or project content — especially when graphify-out/ exists, where the question should be treated as a graphify query first. Turns any input (code, docs, papers, images, videos) into a persistent knowledge graph with god nodes, community detection, and query/path/explain tools.\n- model-routing: Which model to run, why, and what it costs. Load when choosing or changing the session model, when picking a model for a subagent, when asked why a session is expensive, or when the pi-bridge executor comes up. The operative rules are in CLAUDE.md; this is the measured history behind them.\n- design: Create a design canvas — a multi-artboard visual design published as an Artifact that runs Claude Design's canvas editor (an early preview of Claude Design inside Claude Code). You DRAFT the design as .dc.html artboards laid out on one pan/zoom canvas; where saving is enabled for the user's account they refine every element visually (click-to-select, a properties panel, inline text editing, undo/redo) and Save publishes a new version for everyone, otherwise they get a view-and-export (PNG/PDF) preview of your draft. Good for UI mockups and screen flows, landing pages, marketing and social graphics, and print pieces — posters, flyers, brochures as single-page artboards; memos and reports as one flowing artboard. Use when someone wants a design, mockup, wireframe, UI or screen design, landing page, poster, flyer, brochure, banner, card, one-pager, or any visual layout they would rather tweak by hand than in code. Only for CREATING or re-seeding a canvas; an existing one is edited in its published Artifact.\n- dataviz: Use this skill whenever you are about to create ANY chart, graph, plot, dashboard, or data visualization, in ANY output medium — an HTML or React artifact, inline SVG, plotting code in any library (matplotlib, plotly, d3, Recharts, …), an image/PNG you will render and upload, or a chart shared into Slack. Read it BEFORE writing the first line of chart code, choosing chart colors, building a stat tile / meter / KPI row, or laying out a dashboard. Produces visualizations that read as one system — elegant, accessible, consistent in light and dark — using a brand-neutral placeholder palette you swap for your own. Teaches a design-system-agnostic method: a form heuristic, a color formula with a runnable validator, mark specs, and interaction rules. A validated default palette is documented in `references/palette.md` — swap that file's values for your brand's. Triggers on: \"chart\", \"graph\", \"plot\", \"data viz\", \"visualization\", \"dashboard\", \"analytics\", \"visualize data\", \"categorical colors\", \"sequential / diverging palette\", \"stat tile\", \"sparkline\", \"heatmap\", \"legend\", \"axis\", \"tooltip\", \"chart colors\", \"color by series\".\n- artifact-design: Design guidance and fundamentals for Artifacts. - Load before writing any artifact, including Markdown ones — format is part of the design pass, never a speed shortcut.\n- artifact-diagramming: Diagramming know-how for Artifacts — when a picture earns its place, how to draw one that shows the real mechanism, and the inline-SVG mechanics that keep it legible in both themes.\n- artifact-capabilities: Runtime capabilities a published Artifact page can be granted — behavior static HTML cannot provide on its own, such as the page reading live or connected data, keeping state shared across viewers, handing the viewer a file to save, or updating and republishing itself. Serves this user's live capability roster and the typed call definitions. Load it whenever the user asks for an artifact needing any such runtime behavior.\n- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- code-review: Review the current diff, or a PR number/branch/path target, for correctness bugs and reuse/simplification/efficiency cleanups at the given effort level (low/medium: fewer, high-confidence findings; high→max: broader coverage, may include uncertain findings; ultra: deep multi-agent review in the cloud); with no level given, it reuses the level you typed last. Pass --comment to post findings as inline PR comments, or --fix to apply the findings to the working tree after the review. For ultra on a GitHub.com PR target, --post asks to post the finished review’s findings to the PR as a single comment from the user’s GitHub account (not a review; the launch dialog still confirms in interactive sessions, while non-interactive mode posts on the flag alone) and --no-post hides that option.\n- simplify: Review the changed code for reuse, simplification, efficiency, and altitude cleanups, then apply the fixes. Quality only — it does not hunt for bugs; use /code-review for that.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled cloud agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring cloud agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Reference for the Claude API / Anthropic SDK — model ids, pricing, params, streaming, tool use, MCP, agents, caching, token counting, model migration.\nTRIGGER — read BEFORE opening the target file; don't skip because it \"looks like a one-liner\" — whenever: the prompt names Claude/Anthropic in any form (Claude, Anthropic, Fable, Opus, Sonnet, Haiku, `anthropic`, `@anthropic-ai`, `claude-*`, `us.anthropic.*`, `[1m]`); the user asks about an LLM (pricing/model choice/limits/caching) — never answer from memory; OR the task is LLM-shaped with provider unstated (agent/MCP/tool-definition/multi-agent/RAG/LLM-judge/computer-use; generate/summarize/extract/classify/rewrite/converse over NL; debugging refusals/cutoffs/streaming/tool-calls/tokens).\nSKIP only when another provider is being worked on (overrides all triggers): OpenAI/GPT/Gemini/Llama/Mistral/Cohere/Ollama named in the query; OR `grep -rE 'openai|langchain_openai|google.generativeai|genai|mistralai|cohere|ollama'` over the project hits (run this grep FIRST if no provider named — don't Read the file).\n- claude-in-chrome: Automates your Chrome browser to interact with web pages - clicking elements, filling forms, capturing screenshots, reading console logs, and navigating sites. Opens pages in new tabs within your existing Chrome session. Requires site-level permissions before executing (configured in the extension). - When the user wants to interact with web pages, automate browser tasks, capture screenshots, read console logs, or perform any browser-based actions. Always invoke BEFORE attempting to use any mcp__claude-in-chrome__* tools.\n- run: Launch and drive this project's app to see a change working. Use when asked to run, start, or screenshot the app, or to confirm a change works in the real app (not just tests). Systems\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2508.12412\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, here's what I found about the MAST Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy from arXiv 2503.13657:\n\n## Overview\n\nMAST-Data is a comprehensive dataset of 1600+ annotated traces collected across 7 popular MAS frameworks, and the Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy (MAST) was built to enable systematic classification of failures for this dataset.\n\n## Research Motivation\n\nDespite enthusiasm for Multi-Agent LLM Systems (MAS), their performance gains on popular benchmarks are often minimal, highlighting a critical need for a principled understanding of why MAS fail.\n\n## Methodology\n\nMAST was developed through rigorous analysis of 150 traces, guided closely by expert human annotators and validated by high inter-annotator agreement (kappa = 0.88).\n\n## Taxonomy Structure\n\nThe taxonomy identifies 14 unique modes, clustered into 3 categories: (i) system design issues, (ii) inter-agent misalignment, and (iii) task verification.\n\n## Impact\n\nThe paper includes 1,600 annotated traces across 7 frameworks, and the authors found that every framework they tested had failure rates between 41 and 87 percent.\n\nThe paper appears to have been accepted to NeurIPS 2025 Datasets and Benchmarks track and is publicly available on arXiv and GitHub.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"349e3463-c37b-4ef0-8082-3da394612588","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:22:50.351Z","toolUseResult":{"query":"MAST {"parentUuid":"3fb693b4-34ff-436c-a51b-54121fa1790e","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_018ZCCYUJznP8EuXwCR8w5bX","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail taxonomy failure modes percentages\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Why do multi agent LLM systems fail (and how to fix)- 2026 Guide\",\"url\":\"https://futureagi.substack.com/p/why-do-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail\"},{\"title\":\"Think Locally, Explain Globally: Graph-Guided LLM Investigations via Local Reasoning and Belief Propagation\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.17915\"},{\"title\":\"(PDF) Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/389947144_Why_Do_Multi-Agent_LLM_Systems_Fail\"},{\"title\":\"Multi-Agent AI Systems: Why They Fail and How to Fix Coordination Issues (2026) | Augment Code\",\"url\":\"https://www.augmentcode.com/guides/why-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail-and-how-to-fix-them\"},{\"title\":\"MAESTRO: Multi-Agent Evaluation Suite for Testing, Reliability, and Observability\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.00481\"},{\"title\":\"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? | by Anna Alexandra Grigoryan | Medium\",\"url\":\"https://thegrigorian.medium.com/why-do-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail-14dc34e0f3cb\"},{\"title\":\"Paper page - Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\",\"url\":\"https://huggingface.co/papers/2503.13657\"},{\"title\":\"The Six Sigma Agent: Achieving Enterprise-Grade Reliability in LLM Systems Through Consensus-Driven Decomposed Execution\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.22290\"},{\"title\":\"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? Mert Cemri1∗ Melissa Z. Pan1∗ Shuyi Yang2∗\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.13657\"},{\"title\":\"Bridging Human Interpretation and Machine Representation: A Landscape of Qualitative Data Analysis in the LLM Era\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.11739\"}]\n\nBased on the web search results, here's what research shows about why multi-agent LLM systems fail:\n\n## Overall Failure Rates\n\nMulti-agent LLM systems fail 41 to 86% of the time in production, making reliability a significant challenge.\n\n## The MAST Taxonomy\n\nThe MAST failure taxonomy, validated across 1,600+ execution traces at NeurIPS 2025, maps 14 failure modes to three root categories: specification ambiguity, coordination breakdowns, and verification gaps. These fine-grained failure modes are organized into 3 categories: (i) specification and system design failures, (ii) inter-agent misalignment, and (iii) task verification and termination.\n\n## Failure Mode Percentages\n\nThe MAST taxonomy shows that 42% of failures come from bad specifications, 37% from coordination breakdowns, and 21% from weak verification.\n\nA related study breaks down System Design Issues more granularly: System Design Issues account for 44.2%, including Disobey Task Specification (11.8%), Step Repetition (15.7%), and Unaware of Termination Conditions (12.4%).\n\n## Key Insight\n\nMany failures stem from poor system design, not model performance. Agents operate with incorrect assumptions, ignore peer input, or fail to verify their outputs.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"82c56097-43e4-473c-8df1-6c2b511a5d1d","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:22:52.046Z","toolUseResult":{"query":"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail taxonomy failure modes percentages","results":[{"tool_use_id":"srvtoolu_01TH7qwFzooBfMmTUe6TVeMS","content":[{"title":"Why do multi agent LLM systems fail (and how to fix)- 2026 Guide","url":"https://futureagi.substack.com/p/why-do-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail"},{"title":"Think Locally, Explain Globally: Graph-Guided LLM Investigations via Local Reasoning and Belief Propagation","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.17915"},{"title":"(PDF) Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?","url":"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/389947144_Why_Do_Multi-Agent_LLM_Systems_Fail"},{"title":"Multi-Agent AI Systems: Why They Fail and How to Fix Coordination Issues (2026) | Augment Code","url":"https://www.augmentcode.com/guides/why-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail-and-how-to-fix-them"},{"title":"MAESTRO: Multi-Agent Evaluation Suite for Testing, Reliability, and Observability","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.00481"},{"title":"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? | by Anna Alexandra Grigoryan | Medium","url":"https://thegrigorian.medium.com/why-do-multi-agent-llm-systems-fail-14dc34e0f3cb"},{"title":"Paper page - Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?","url":"https://huggingface.co/papers/2503.13657"},{"title":"The Six Sigma Agent: Achieving Enterprise-Grade Reliability in LLM Systems Through Consensus-Driven Decomposed Execution","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.22290"},{"title":"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? {"parentUuid":"d4ce74b8-e127-4a87-a018-0bed1de37b09","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01BpQvqpuXRMuhX1TKBS15ho","type":"tool_result","content":"# Research Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Despite enthusiasm for Multi-Agent LLM Systems (MAS), their performance gains on popular benchmarks are often minimal. This gap highlights a critical need for a principled understanding of why MAS fail. Addressing this question requires systematic identification and analysis of failure patterns. We introduce MAST-Data, a comprehensive dataset of 1600+ annotated traces collected across 7 popular MAS frameworks. MAST-Data is the first multi-agent system dataset to outline the failure dynamics in MAS for guiding the development of better future systems. To enable systematic classification of failures for MAST-Data, we build the first Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy (MAST). We develop MAST through rigorous analysis of 150 traces, guided closely by expert human annotators and validated by high inter-annotator agreement (kappa = 0.88). This process identifies 14 unique modes, clustered into 3 categories: (i) system design issues, (ii) inter-agent misalignment, and (iii) task verification. To enable scalable annotation, we develop an LLM-as-a-Judge pipeline with high agreement with human annotations. We leverage MAST and MAST-Data to analyze failure patterns across models (GPT4, Claude 3, Qwen2.5, CodeLlama) and tasks (coding, math, general agent), demonstrating improvement headrooms from better MAS design. Our analysis provides insights revealing that identified failures require more sophisticated solutions, highlighting a clear roadmap for future research. We publicly release our comprehensive dataset (MAST-Data), the MAST, and our LLM annotator to facilitate widespread research and development in MAS.\"\n\n**Authors:** Mert Cemri, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang, Lakshya A. Agrawal, Bhavya Chopra, Rishabh Tiwari, Kurt Keutzer, Aditya Parameswaran, Dan Klein, Kannan Ramchandran, Matei Zaharia, Joseph E. Gonzalez, Ion Stoica\n\n**Venue:** arXiv (cs.AI)\n\n**Key Metrics:**\n- 1,600+ annotated traces\n- 7 frameworks analyzed\n- 150 traces for taxonomy development\n- Inter-annotator agreement (kappa): 0.88\n- 14 failure modes across 3 categories\n- Specific improvement percentages: Not provided in abstract"}]},"uuid":"5c87c0c6-767f-4a66-90fe-87a4f1173217","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:23:07.222Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":44175,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Research Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Despite enthusiasm for Multi-Agent LLM Systems (MAS), their performance gains on popular benchmarks are often minimal. This gap highlights a critical need for a principled understanding of why MAS fail. Addressing this question requires systematic identification and analysis of failure patterns. We introduce MAST-Data, a comprehensive dataset of 1600+ annotated traces collected across 7 popular MAS frameworks. MAST-Data is the first multi-agent system dataset to outline the failure dynamics in MAS for guiding the development of better future systems. To enable systematic classification of failures for MAST-Data, we build the first Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy (MAST). We develop MAST through rigorous analysis of 150 traces, guided closely by expert human annotators and validated by high inter-annotator agreement (kappa = 0.88). This process identifies 14 unique modes, clustered into 3 categories: (i) system design issues, (ii) inter-agent misalignment, and (iii) task verification. To enable scalable annotation, we develop an LLM-as-a-Judge pipeline with high agreement with human annotations. We leverage MAST and MAST-Data to analyze failure patterns across models (GPT4, Claude 3, Qwen2.5, CodeLlama) and tasks (coding, math, general agent), demonstrating improvement headrooms from better MAS design. Our analysis provides insights revealing that identified failures require more sophisticated solutions, highlighting a clear roadmap for future research. We publicly release our comprehensive dataset (MAST-Data), the MAST, and our LLM annotator to facilitate widespread research and development in MAS.\"\n\n**Authors:** Mert Cemri, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang, Lakshya A. Agrawal, Bhavya Chopra, Rishabh Tiwari, Kurt Keutzer, Aditya Parameswaran, Dan Klein, Kannan Ramchandran, Matei Zaharia, Joseph E. No guessing.\n\nSpecific things to chase (leads, not limits):\n- Error propagation / compounding error in multi-agent or multi-step LLM pipelines: measured per-step degradation, e.g. papers on \"cascading failures\", \"error propagation in LLM agent pipelines\", compounding error rates over N steps.\n- Telephone-game / iterative-rewriting degradation: measured information loss when an LLM summarises then re-summarises, or when a spec is relayed agent-to-agent. Look for \"LLM telephone game\", \"cultural transmission chains LLM\", \"iterated learning LLM\", \"semantic drift in LLM chains\", \"attractor\" studies.\n- Context degradation: \"Lost in the Middle\" (Liu et al., TACL 2024), \"Lost in Conversation\"/\"LLMs Get Lost in Multi-Turn Conversation\" (Microsoft Research 2025 — I believe reports a large measured drop, get the exact %), context rot / long-context degradation measurements.\n- Instruction-following degradation over turns: multi-turn instruction benchmarks (e.g. Multi-IF, SIFo, IFEval multi-turn) with measured drop per turn.\n- The MAST paper (arXiv 2503.13657) category \"inter-agent misalignment\" sub-numbers — I already have those, so do NOT re-derive; only add anything about how they were measured.\n- Any measurement of underspecification / ambiguous task specs causing agent failure.\n\nRETURN:\n1. FINDINGS: each = the number, exact source (title, venue/year, arXiv id or URL), one line on the implication for designing a divide-and-conquer agent protocol where a parent decomposes a spec and hands pieces to children.\n2. Anything unsourced gets marked \"unverifiable\".\n\nChase both directions:\n\nEVIDENCE IT HELPS:\n- Verifier / reward-model reranking gains: \"Let's Verify Step by Step\" (Lightman et al., OpenAI, ICLR 2024) process vs outcome supervision numbers; \"Training Verifiers to Solve Math Word Problems\" (Cobbe et al. 2021, GSM8K) verification vs finetuning numbers.\n- Generator-verifier gap / \"verification is easier than generation\" measurements.\n- CriticGPT (OpenAI 2024) — measured preference rate of critiques, bug catch rate.\n- Multi-agent debate / actor-critic setups with measured accuracy gains, e.g. Du et al. multiagent debate, CAMEL, \"Self-Refine\" (NeurIPS 2023) gains.\n- Test-time verification/best-of-n scaling measurements.\n- The MAST paper's own intervention result (+15.6% on ProgramDev from adding verification) — confirm it if you can, do not re-derive.\n- Anything on INDEPENDENT/adversarial verifier vs self-verification (same model reviewing itself).\n\nEVIDENCE IT DOES NOT HELP:\n- \"Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet\" (Huang et al., ICLR 2024) — the measured drops from intrinsic self-correction.\n- \"Self-critique\"/self-refinement failure measurements; sycophancy in LLM judges; LLM-as-judge bias measurements (position bias, self-preference bias — e.g. \"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations\").\n- Any measurement showing a critic stage degrades correct answers (flipping right to wrong).\n\nRETURN:\n1. (Here's What Worked)\",\"url\":\"https://fountaincity.tech/resources/blog/anthropic-multi-agent-blueprint-production/\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found comprehensive information about Anthropic's multi-agent research system and the 15x token usage metric you asked about.\n\n## Key Findings\n\n**Architecture**: Anthropic built their multi-agent research system using an orchestrator-worker pattern, where the Lead Researcher agent acts as the main coordinator and subagents handle individual parts of the job.\n\n**Token Usage**: Agents typically use approximately 4× more tokens than chat interactions, and multi-agent systems use about 15× more tokens than chats.\n\n**Performance Gains**: In internal evaluations, a system with Claude Opus 4 as the lead agent and Claude Sonnet 4 as supporting subagents outperformed a single-agent setup by more than 90 percent.\n\n**Performance Drivers**: Three factors explained 95% of performance variance: token usage (80%), number of tool calls (~10%), and model choice (~5%).\n\n**Economic Trade-off**: For economic viability, multi-agent systems require tasks where the value is high enough to justify the increased performance costs.\n\n**Best Use Cases**: The sweet spot for multi-agent systems is valuable tasks involving heavy parallelization, information exceeding single context windows, and interfacing with numerous complex tools.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown Debate\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.00914\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found several relevant papers on arXiv that discuss multi-agent debate, token cost scaling, and diminishing returns with agent count:\n\n## Key Findings:\n\n**Multi-Agent Scaling and Diminishing Returns:**\n\nPerformance generally improves as more agents are added to debate, but with diminishing returns, with the most significant gains occurring when moving from a single agent to two agents, with more modest improvements as additional agents join the debate.\n\nMultiAgentBench benchmarks show that additional agents exhibit clear diminishing marginal returns.\n\n**Token Cost Escalation:**\n\nThe escalation in the number of agents and debate rounds can drastically raise the tokens cost of debates, thereby limiting the scalability of the multi-agent debate technique.\n\nUnder 4 rounds, as the number of agents increases from 1 to 8, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively grown by 36×, 44×, 49×.\n\n**Cost-Benefit Analysis:**\n\nThe debate architecture imposes a 2.1×-3.4× token cost multiplier over isolated self-correction to achieve accuracy that is statistically comparable to, or worse than, non-communicative baselines.\n\nMulti-agent scaling advantage is maximized in parallelizable, structured benchmarks, with rapidly diminishing or negative returns in open-ended or highly sequential environments.\n\nThese papers provide quantitative measurements of how token costs scale non-linearly with agent count while performance gains plateau, highlighting a critical trade-off in multi-agent debate systems.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown {"parentUuid":"6d13799f-24e8-47bd-a2ac-d28bcbf224b1","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01W1vvFkeHCV9Uku8dpCP1NF","type":"tool_result","content":"# GroupDebate Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse NLP tasks. Extensive research has explored how to enhance the logical reasoning abilities such as Chain-of-Thought, Chain-of-Thought with Self-Consistency, Tree-Of-Thoughts, and multi-agent debates. In the context of multi-agent debates, significant performance improvements can be achieved with an increasing number of agents and debate rounds. However, the escalation in the number of agents and debate rounds can drastically raise the tokens cost of debates, thereby limiting the scalability of the multi-agent debate technique. To better harness the advantages of multi-agent debates in logical reasoning tasks, this paper proposes a method to significantly reduce token cost in multi-agent debates. This approach involves dividing all agents into multiple debate groups, with agents engaging in debates within their respective groups and sharing interim debate results between groups. Comparative experiments across multiple datasets have demonstrated that this method can reduce the total tokens by up to 51.7% during debates and while potentially enhancing accuracy by as much as 25%. Our method significantly enhances the performance and efficiency of interactions in the multi-agent debate.\"\n\n**Performance Metrics:**\nThe document reports that GroupDebate achieves \"up to 51.7%\" token reduction and potential accuracy improvement of \"as much as 25%\" compared to baseline multi-agent debate approaches. However, specific token-cost scaling data for 1-8 agents across GSM8K, Arithmetic, and MMLU datasets is not provided in this page content.\n\n**Venue & Year:** AAMAS 2026"}]},"uuid":"a25e4b78-4ddc-4877-9a9a-669b4b39711f","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:25:27.618Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":42790,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# GroupDebate Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"In recent years, Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse NLP tasks. In the context of multi-agent debates, significant performance improvements can be achieved with an increasing number of agents and debate rounds. However, the escalation in the number of agents and debate rounds can drastically raise the tokens cost of debates, thereby limiting the scalability of the multi-agent debate technique. To better harness the advantages of multi-agent debates in logical reasoning tasks, this paper proposes a method to significantly reduce token cost in multi-agent debates. This approach involves dividing all agents into multiple debate groups, with agents engaging in debates within their respective groups and sharing interim debate results between groups. Comparative experiments across multiple datasets have demonstrated that this method can reduce the total tokens by up to 51.7% during debates and while potentially enhancing accuracy by as much as 25%. {"parentUuid":"5283ec03-5576-4496-b150-b6b5940d86ad","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ML9JPu4crK6mUC55RUx7dj","type":"tool_result","content":"# Token Cost Growth Analysis from GroupDebate Paper\n\n## Growth with Increasing Agents (4 rounds)\n\nFrom Section 2.2:\n\n\"under 4 rounds, as the number of agents increases from 1 to 8, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively grown by 36×/44×/49×\"\n\n## Growth with Increasing Rounds (4 agents)\n\nFrom the same section:\n\n\"under 4 agents, as the number of rounds increases from 1 to 4, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively increased by 17×/29×/19×\"\n\n## Performance Improvements (GD vs MAD)\n\nTable 1 shows token reduction and accuracy gains for GD(5,3) versus MAD(5,3):\n\n**Arithmetic**: 20.9% token reduction (12.1k → 9.6k tokens); 5.2% accuracy gain (94.4% → 99.6%)\n\n**GSM8K**: 24.8% token reduction (13.7k → 10.3k tokens); slight decrease (82.0% → 81.6%)\n\n**MMLU**: 30.6% token reduction (16.0k → 11.1k tokens); 6.1% accuracy gain (68.8% → 74.9%)\n\n**MATH**: 14.0% token reduction (16.5k → 13.7k tokens); decrease (39.0% → 37.8%)\n\n**GPQA**: 13.6% token reduction (42.7k → 36.9k tokens); 1.4% accuracy gain (31.2% → 32.6%)"}]},"uuid":"233931c6-7794-48c0-aaa6-a019c685d3bc","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:25:41.224Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":419366,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Token Cost Growth Analysis from GroupDebate Paper\n\n## Growth with Increasing Agents (4 rounds)\n\nFrom Section 2.2:\n\n\"under 4 rounds, as the number of agents increases from 1 to 8, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively grown by 36×/44×/49×\"\n\n## Growth with Increasing Rounds (4 agents)\n\nFrom the same section:\n\n\"under 4 agents, as the number of rounds increases from 1 to 4, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively increased by 17×/29×/19×\"\n\n## Performance Improvements (GD vs MAD)\n\nTable 1 shows token reduction and accuracy gains for GD(5,3) versus MAD(5,3):\n\n**Arithmetic**: 20.9% token reduction (12.1k → 9.6k tokens); 5.2% accuracy gain (94.4% → 99.6%)\n\n**GSM8K**: 24.8% token reduction (13.7k → 10.3k tokens); slight decrease (82.0% → 81.6%)\n\n**MMLU**: 30.6% token reduction (16.0k → 11.1k tokens); 6.1% accuracy gain (68.8% → 74.9%)\n\n**MATH**: 14.0% token reduction (16.5k → 13.7k tokens); decrease (39.0% → 37.8%)\n\n**GPQA**: 13.6% token reduction (42.7k → 36.9k tokens); 1.4% accuracy gain (31.2% → 32.6%)","durationMs":6542,"url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2409.14051v2"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"5283ec03-5576-4496-b150-b6b5940d86ad","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}
{"parentUuid":"9be3b0a6-68f1-4d06-8d23-a25d7950a1c6","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Ka1eTB4skhCTwbu9X8481z","type":"tool_result","content":"# Key Findings from \"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\"\n\n## (a) Failure Rates Across MAS Frameworks\n\nThe paper presents failure rates in **Figure 1** but does not provide a single consolidated table with all seven frameworks' rates. The document states:\n\n> \"ChatDev ([Qian et al. 2023](#bib.bib65)) achieves only 33.33% correctness on our ProgramDev benchmark\"\n\nThe seven analyzed MAS frameworks are: MetaGPT, ChatDev, HyperAgent, AppWorld, AG2, Magentic-One, and OpenManus. However, individual correctness percentages are not consistently tabulated across all systems in comparable metrics.\n\n## (b) Distribution of 14 Failure Modes\n\n**Figure 2** provides the taxonomy distribution. Key percentages include:\n- FM-1.3 (Step repetition): 17.14%\n- FM-2.6 (Reasoning-action mismatch): 13.98%\n- FM-2.2 (Fail to ask clarification): 11.65%\n- FM-1.1 (Disobey task specification): 10.98%\n\nOverall category breakdown: FC1 (Specification): 41.77%, FC2 (Inter-Agent): 36.94%, FC3 (Verification): 21.30%\n\n## (c) Case Study Interventions and Results\n\nTwo case studies applied prompt and architectural modifications:\n\n**AG2 - MathChat**: +9.4% success rate improvement\n**ChatDev**: +15.6% absolute improvement on ProgramDev following verification enhancements\n\nBaseline: ChatDev's initial 33.33% correctness on ProgramDev\n\n## (d) LLM-as-a-Judge Performance\n\n**Table 2** shows:\n- Accuracy: 94%\n- Cohen's κ (kappa): 0.77 (with few-shot examples)\n- Generalization to unseen systems: Cohen's κ = 0.79\n\nHuman inter-annotator agreement achieved Cohen's κ = 0.88."}]},"uuid":"96649e56-b979-4ad4-a937-7c3381dfa7b0","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:25:45.525Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":1667401,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Key Findings from \"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\"\n\n## (a) Failure Rates Across MAS Frameworks\n\nThe paper presents failure rates in **Figure 1** but does not provide a single consolidated table with all seven frameworks' rates. The document states:\n\n> \"ChatDev ([Qian et al. 2023](#bib.bib65)) achieves only 33.33% correctness on our ProgramDev benchmark\"\n\nThe seven analyzed MAS frameworks are: MetaGPT, ChatDev, HyperAgent, AppWorld, AG2, Magentic-One, and OpenManus. However, individual correctness percentages are not consistently tabulated across all systems in comparable metrics.\n\n## (b) Distribution of 14 Failure Modes\n\n**Figure 2** provides the taxonomy distribution. Key percentages include:\n- FM-1.3 (Step repetition): 17.14%\n- FM-2.6 (Reasoning-action mismatch): 13.98%\n- FM-2.2 (Fail to ask clarification): 11.65%\n- FM-1.1 (Disobey task specification): 10.98%\n\nOverall category breakdown: FC1 (Specification): 41.77%, FC2 (Inter-Agent): 36.94%, FC3 (Verification): 21.30%\n\n## (c) Case Study Interventions and Results\n\nTwo case studies applied prompt and architectural modifications:\n\n**AG2 - MathChat**: +9.4% success rate improvement\n**ChatDev**: +15.6% absolute improvement on ProgramDev following verification enhancements\n\nBaseline: ChatDev's initial 33.33% correctness on ProgramDev\n\n## (d) LLM-as-a-Judge Performance\n\n**Table 2** shows:\n- Accuracy: 94%\n- Cohen's κ (kappa): 0.77 (with few-shot examples)\n- Generalization to unseen systems: Cohen's κ = 0.79\n\nHuman inter-annotator agreement achieved Cohen's κ = 0.88.","durationMs":8338,"url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2503.13657v2"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"9be3b0a6-68f1-4d06-8d23-a25d7950a1c6","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

Yingbo Zhou, and Jennifer Neville from Microsoft Research and Salesforce Research\n\n**Key Finding:** All the top open- and closed-weight LLMs tested exhibit significantly lower performance in multi-turn conversations than single-turn, with an average drop of 39% across six generation tasks.\n\n**Research Approach:** The work performs large-scale simulation experiments to compare LLM performance in single- and multi-turn settings.\n\n**Main Discovery:** When LLMs take a wrong turn in a conversation, they get lost and do not recover.\n\n**Publication:** The paper was submitted to arXiv on May 9, 2025 (arXiv:2505.06120)\n\n**Resources:** There is a code repository to facilitate benchmarking LLMs on multi-turn task completion and reproduction of experiments included in the {"parentUuid":"de609ea1-a343-42f9-acf7-3e9cfefd80b7","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01DGbyM2RZnnQZkZwGrRVqSN","type":"tool_result","content":"# Analysis of Scaling Agent Systems Paper\n\n## Abstract (Verbatim)\n\"Agents, language model-based systems capable of reasoning, planning, and acting are widely adopted in real-world tasks, yet how their performance changes as these systems scale across key dimensions remains underexplored. We introduce quantitative scaling principles for agent systems as a predictive model, capturing how performance varies with coordination, model capability, and measurable system and task factors. Across 260 configurations spanning six agentic benchmarks, five canonical architectures (Single-Agent and four Multi-Agent: Independent, Centralized, Decentralized, Hybrid), and three LLM families, we perform controlled evaluations, standardizing tools, prompts, and compute to isolate architectural effects. The resulting model achieves a cross-validated R^2=0.373 across all six benchmarks (R^2=0.413 with a task-grounded capability metric). We identify a robust capability-saturation effect and additional patterns: (1) a coordination yields diminishing returns once single-agent baselines exceed certain performance; (2) tool-heavy tasks appear to incur multi-agent overhead; and (3) architectures without centralized verification tend to propagate errors more than those with centralized coordination. Relative performance change compared to single-agent baseline ranges from +80.8% on decomposable financial reasoning to -70.0% on sequential planning, demonstrating that architecture-task alignment determines collaborative success.\"\n\n## Venue/Year\narXiv preprint, submitted December 2025 (latest version April 2026)\n\n## Key Quantitative Findings on Scaling\n\n**Performance bounds:** Performance ranges from \"+80.8% on decomposable financial reasoning to -70.0% on sequential planning\" versus single-agent baseline.\n\n**Model fit:** \"cross-validated R^2=0.373 across all six benchmarks\"\n\n**Saturation pattern:** The authors identify \"a robust capability-saturation effect\" and note that \"coordination yields diminishing returns once single-agent baselines exceed certain performance.\"\n\n**Overhead effects:** \"tool-heavy tasks appear to incur multi-agent overhead\" and architectures lacking centralized verification \"propagate errors more\" than coordinated alternatives.\n\nThe paper does not explicitly provide scaling exponents or saturation point values in the abstract."}]},"uuid":"0cc7be45-6a7c-4ffc-beb1-a83fa046445b","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:05.842Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":45377,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Analysis of Scaling Agent Systems Paper\n\n## Abstract (Verbatim)\n\"Agents, language model-based systems capable of reasoning, planning, and acting are widely adopted in real-world tasks, yet how their performance changes as these systems scale across key dimensions remains underexplored. We introduce quantitative scaling principles for agent systems as a predictive model, capturing how performance varies with coordination, model capability, and measurable system and task factors. Across 260 configurations spanning six agentic benchmarks, five canonical architectures (Single-Agent and four Multi-Agent: Independent, Centralized, Decentralized, Hybrid), and three LLM families, we perform controlled evaluations, standardizing tools, prompts, and compute to isolate architectural effects. The resulting model achieves a cross-validated R^2=0.373 across all six benchmarks (R^2=0.413 with a task-grounded capability metric). We identify a robust capability-saturation effect and additional patterns: (1) a coordination yields diminishing returns once single-agent baselines exceed certain performance; (2) tool-heavy tasks appear to incur multi-agent overhead; and (3) architectures without centralized verification tend to propagate errors more than those with centralized coordination. Relative performance change compared to single-agent baseline ranges from +80.8% on decomposable financial reasoning to -70.0% on sequential planning, demonstrating that architecture-task alignment determines collaborative success.\"\n\n## Venue/Year\narXiv preprint, submitted December 2025 (latest version April 2026)\n\n## Key Quantitative Findings on Scaling\n\n**Performance bounds:** Performance ranges from \"+80.8% on decomposable financial reasoning to -70.0% on sequential planning\" versus single-agent baseline.\n\n**Model fit:** \"cross-validated R^2=0.373 across all six benchmarks\"\n\n**Saturation pattern:** The authors identify \"a robust capability-saturation effect\" and note that \"coordination yields diminishing returns once single-agent baselines exceed certain performance.\"\n\n**Overhead effects:** \"tool-heavy tasks appear to incur multi-agent overhead\" and architectures lacking centralized verification \"propagate errors more\" than coordinated alternatives.\n\nThe paper does not explicitly provide scaling exponents or saturation point values in the abstract.","durationMs":7560,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"de609ea1-a343-42f9-acf7-3e9cfefd80b7","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

{"parentUuid":"4a1309a9-5d0d-4af6-9fbb-9432d999970b","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HBJPYz6d12UYNKjmKAAAmL","type":"tool_result","content":"# Problem Drift in Multi-Agent Debate: Key Findings\n\n## Abstract\n\"Multi-agent debate - multiple instances of large language models discussing problems in turn-based interaction - has shown promise for solving knowledge and reasoning tasks.\"\n\n## Venue & Year\nEACL 2026\n\n## Measured Numbers on Problem Drift\n\n**Drift Frequency by Task Type:**\n- Generative tasks: 76-89% drift occurrence\n- High-complexity tasks: 7-21% drift occurrence\n\n**Root Causes (from 170 analyzed debates):**\n- Lack of progress: 35% of cases\n- Low-quality feedback: 26% of cases\n- Lack of clarity: 25% of cases\n\n**Mitigation Results:**\n- DRIFTPolicy addresses 31% of problem drift cases\n\n**Detection Method:**\nThe paper proposes DRIFTJudge, an LLM-as-a-judge approach as a baseline for detecting drift, though specific accuracy metrics are not provided in the abstract or header information shown.\n\n**Study Scope:**\nAnalysis covered ten tasks across four categories: three generative, three knowledge-based, three reasoning, and one instruction-following task."}]},"uuid":"5ef65667-9722-4bdf-8c45-f6ce599a515a","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:16.191Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":42526,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Problem Drift in Multi-Agent Debate: Key Findings\n\n## Abstract\n\"Multi-agent debate - multiple instances of large language models discussing problems in turn-based interaction - has shown promise for solving knowledge and reasoning tasks.\"\n\n## Venue & Year\nEACL 2026\n\n## Measured Numbers on Problem Drift\n\n**Drift Frequency by Task Type:**\n- Generative tasks: 76-89% drift occurrence\n- High-complexity tasks: 7-21% drift occurrence\n\n**Root Causes (from 170 analyzed debates):**\n- Lack of progress: 35% of cases\n- Low-quality feedback: 26% of cases\n- Lack of clarity: 25% of cases\n\n**Mitigation Results:**\n- DRIFTPolicy addresses 31% of problem drift cases\n\n**Detection Method:**\nThe paper proposes DRIFTJudge, an LLM-as-a-judge approach as a baseline for detecting drift, though specific accuracy metrics are not provided in the abstract or header information shown.\n\n**Study Scope:**\nAnalysis covered ten tasks across four categories: three generative, three knowledge-based, three reasoning, and one instruction-following task.","durationMs":4380,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19559"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"4a1309a9-5d0d-4af6-9fbb-9432d999970b","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

{"parentUuid":"7a78e2b6-9b94-45bf-893b-dd99115ae137","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01E4FCwMmjtH95jGykESUks7","type":"tool_result","content":"# Paper Extract\n\n**Abstract:**\n\"Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as a groundbreaking technology with their unparalleled text generation capabilities across various applications. Nevertheless, concerns persist regarding the accuracy and appropriateness of their generated content. A contemporary methodology, self-correction, has been proposed as a remedy to these issues. Building upon this premise, this paper critically examines the role and efficacy of self-correction within LLMs, shedding light on its true potential and limitations. Central to our investigation is the notion of intrinsic self-correction, whereby an LLM attempts to correct its initial responses based solely on its inherent capabilities, without the crutch of external feedback. In the context of reasoning, our research indicates that LLMs struggle to self-correct their responses without external feedback, and at times, their performance even degrades after self-correction. Drawing from these insights, we offer suggestions for future research and practical applications in this field.\"\n\n**Venue:** ICLR 2024\n\n**Year:** 2024 (submitted October 2023, revised March 2024)\n\n---\n\n## Measured Numbers on Intrinsic Self-Correction\n\nThe provided webpage content contains only the abstract and metadata. Specific numerical measurements regarding accuracy before/after self-correction on GSM8K, CommonSenseQA, HotpotQA, error transitions, and multi-agent debate comparisons are **not included** in this excerpt. Access to the full PDF or HTML version would be necessary to extract those quantitative results."}]},"uuid":"2f5f9ba7-d241-4cb0-87ab-c9387612edfc","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:30.098Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":41575,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Paper Extract\n\n**Abstract:**\n\"Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as a groundbreaking technology with their unparalleled text generation capabilities across various applications. Nevertheless, concerns persist regarding the accuracy and appropriateness of their generated content. A contemporary methodology, self-correction, has been proposed as a remedy to these issues. Building upon this premise, this paper critically examines the role and efficacy of self-correction within LLMs, shedding light on its true potential and limitations. Central to our investigation is the notion of intrinsic self-correction, whereby an LLM attempts to correct its initial responses based solely on its inherent capabilities, without the crutch of external feedback. In the context of reasoning, our research indicates that LLMs struggle to self-correct their responses without external feedback, and at times, their performance even degrades after self-correction. Drawing from these insights, we offer suggestions for future research and practical applications in this field.\"\n\n**Venue:** ICLR 2024\n\n**Year:** 2024 (submitted October 2023, revised March 2024)\n\n---\n\n## Measured Numbers on Intrinsic Self-Correction\n\nThe provided webpage content contains only the abstract and metadata. Access to the full PDF or HTML version would be necessary to extract those quantitative results.","durationMs":5037,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"7a78e2b6-9b94-45bf-893b-dd99115ae137","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}
{"parentUuid":"a46c6bba-6eb9-4148-8ccc-f21f254c623d","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01K2UZP6vNVuXmriCsMzx9te","type":"tool_result","content":"# Paper Summary: \"Let's Verify Step by Step\"\n\n**Venue & Year:** arXiv (Computer Science > Machine Learning), submitted May 31, 2023\n\n**Abstract:**\n\"In recent years, large language models have greatly improved in their ability to perform complex multi-step reasoning. However, even state-of-the-art models still regularly produce logical mistakes. To train more reliable models, we can turn either to outcome supervision, which provides feedback for a final result, or process supervision, which provides feedback for each intermediate reasoning step. Given the importance of training reliable models, and given the high cost of human feedback, it is important to carefully compare the both methods. Recent work has already begun this comparison, but many questions still remain. We conduct our own investigation, finding that process supervision significantly outperforms outcome supervision for training models to solve problems from the challenging MATH dataset. Our process-supervised model solves 78% of problems from a representative subset of the MATH test set. Additionally, we show that active learning significantly improves the efficacy of process supervision. To support related research, we also release PRM800K, the complete dataset of 800,000 step-level human feedback labels used to train our best reward model.\"\n\n**Measured Results:**\n- Process-supervised model accuracy: **78%** of problems solved on MATH test subset\n- Dataset released: **PRM800K** (800,000 step-level feedback labels)\n\nNote: The page content does not provide specific comparative accuracy numbers for outcome-supervised models or majority voting baselines, nor does it mention best-of-N sample sizes."}]},"uuid":"def34344-6c1f-4952-b115-aa7dce47bcef","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:31.382Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":42592,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Paper Summary: \"Let's Verify Step by Step\"\n\n**Venue & Year:** arXiv (Computer Science > Machine Learning), submitted May 31, 2023\n\n**Abstract:**\n\"In recent years, large language models have greatly improved in their ability to perform complex multi-step reasoning. However, even state-of-the-art models still regularly produce logical mistakes. To train more reliable models, we can turn either to outcome supervision, which provides feedback for a final result, or process supervision, which provides feedback for each intermediate reasoning step. Given the importance of training reliable models, and given the high cost of human feedback, it is important to carefully compare the both methods. Recent work has already begun this comparison, but many questions still remain. We conduct our own investigation, finding that process supervision significantly outperforms outcome supervision for training models to solve problems from the challenging MATH dataset. Our process-supervised model solves 78% of problems from a representative subset of the MATH test set. Additionally, we show that active learning significantly improves the efficacy of process supervision. To support related research, we also release PRM800K, the complete dataset of 800,000 step-level human feedback labels used to train our best reward model.\"\n\n**Measured Results:**\n- Process-supervised model accuracy: **78%** of problems solved on MATH test subset\n- Dataset released: **PRM800K** (800,000 step-level feedback labels)\n\nNote: The page content does not provide specific comparative accuracy numbers for outcome-supervised models or majority voting baselines, nor does it mention best-of-N sample sizes.","durationMs":5482,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.20050"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"a46c6bba-6eb9-4148-8ccc-f21f254c623d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

Best-of-N Results Comparison\n\nBased on Figure 3 in the paper, here are the exact results at best-of-1860:\n\n- **PRM (Process-Supervised)**: 78.2%\n- **ORM (Outcome-Supervised)**: 72.4%\n- **Majority Voting**: 69.6%\n\nThe paper states: \"While the ORM performs slightly better than the majority voting baseline, the PRM strongly outperforms both.\"\n\nThe key finding is that the performance gap between PRM and ORM \"widens as N increases,\" demonstrating the PRM's superior effectiveness at searching over larger numbers of model-generated solutions."}]},"uuid":"1f0d9471-a893-4c66-bab0-d7024507429b","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:47.976Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":134992,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Best-of-N Results 69.6%\n\nThe paper states: \"While the ORM performs slightly better than the majority voting baseline, the PRM strongly outperforms both.\"\n\nThe key finding is that the performance gap between PRM {"parentUuid":"dd5b2cb0-fa04-4847-a16d-da9485b94d24","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01U5BLkgpD4Pajvp59nHYZwy","type":"tool_result","content":"# Key Findings from Self-Correction Study\n\n## Table 2: Self-Correction with Oracle Labels\n\n| Model | GSM8K | CommonSenseQA | HotpotQA |\n|-------|-------|---------------|----------|\n| GPT-3.5 Standard | 75.9% | 75.8% | 26.0% |\n| GPT-3.5 Self-Correct (Oracle) | 84.3% | 89.7% | 29.0% |\n| GPT-4 Standard | 95.5% | 82.0% | 49.0% |\n| GPT-4 Self-Correct (Oracle) | 97.5% | 85.5% | 59.0% |\n\n## Table 3: Intrinsic Self-Correction Results\n\n**GPT-3.5** showed consistent performance degradation:\n- GSM8K: 75.9% → 75.1% (round 1) → 74.7% (round 2)\n- CommonSenseQA: 75.8% → 38.1% (round 1) → 41.8% (round 2)\n- HotpotQA: 26.0% → 25.0% → 25.0%\n\n**GPT-4** also declined across benchmarks, particularly on CommonSenseQA (82.0% → 79.5% → 80.0%).\n\n## Answer Change Analysis\n\nFor GSM8K with GPT-3.5: \"74.7% of the time, GPT-3.5 retains its initial answer.\" Among changes, the model was \"more likely to modify a correct answer to an incorrect one\" than the reverse.\n\n## Multi-Agent Debate vs. Self-Consistency\n\n| Method | Responses | GSM8K Accuracy |\n|--------|-----------|-----------------|\n| Self-Consistency (baseline) | 3 | 82.5% |\n| Multi-Agent Debate (round 1) | 6 | 83.2% |\n| Self-Consistency | 6 | 85.3% |\n| Multi-Agent Debate (round 2) | 9 | 83.0% |\n| Self-Consistency | 9 | 88.2% |\n\n**Conclusion**: \"Multi-agent debate significantly underperforms simple self-consistency\" when comparing equivalent response counts."}]},"uuid":"7003dab5-e8a4-4df2-a272-d42d51472408","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:26:51.351Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":447510,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Key Findings from Self-Correction Study\n\n## Table 2: Self-Correction with Oracle Labels\n\n| Model | GSM8K | CommonSenseQA | HotpotQA |\n|-------|-------|---------------|----------|\n| GPT-3.5 Standard | 75.9% | 75.8% | 26.0% |\n| GPT-3.5 Self-Correct (Oracle) | 84.3% | 89.7% | 29.0% |\n| GPT-4 Standard | 95.5% | 82.0% | 49.0% |\n| GPT-4 Self-Correct (Oracle) | 97.5% | 85.5% | 59.0% |\n\n## Table 3: Intrinsic Self-Correction Results\n\n**GPT-3.5** showed consistent performance degradation:\n- GSM8K: 75.9% → 75.1% (round 1) → 74.7% (round 2)\n- CommonSenseQA: 75.8% → 38.1% (round 1) → 41.8% (round 2)\n- HotpotQA: 26.0% → 25.0% → 25.0%\n\n**GPT-4** also declined across benchmarks, particularly on CommonSenseQA (82.0% → 79.5% → 80.0%).\n\n## Answer Change Analysis\n\nFor GSM8K with GPT-3.5: \"74.7% of the time, GPT-3.5 retains its initial answer.\" Among changes, the model was \"more likely to modify a correct answer to an incorrect one\" than the reverse.\n\n## Multi-Agent Debate vs. Self-Consistency\n\n| Method | Responses | GSM8K Accuracy |\n|--------|-----------|-----------------|\n| Self-Consistency (baseline) | 3 | 82.5% |\n| Multi-Agent Debate (round 1) | 6 | 83.2% |\n| MATS Research\",\"url\":\"https://www.matsprogram.org/research/llm-evaluators-recognize-and-favor-their-own-generations\"},{\"title\":\"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2404.13076\"},{\"title\":\"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations Arjun Panickssery1\",\"url\":\"https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/file/7f1f0218e45f5414c79c0679633e47bc-Paper-Conference.pdf\"},{\"title\":\"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations — EA Forum\",\"url\":\"https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/kwdGGqfLnB8RRs6ki/llm-evaluators-recognize-and-favor-their-own-generations\"},{\"title\":\"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations\",\"url\":\"https://www.researchgate.net/publication/397200002_LLM_Evaluators_Recognize_and_Favor_Their_Own_Generations\"},{\"title\":\"LLM evaluators recognize and favor their own generations | Proceedings of the 38th International Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems\",\"url\":\"https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.5555/3737916.3740113\"}]\n\nI found comprehensive information about this research. Here's what the search results show:\n\n## Key Findings\n\nSelf-preference is a bias where an LLM evaluator scores its own outputs higher than others' while human annotators consider them of equal quality.\n\nThe research investigates whether LLMs actually recognize their own outputs when giving them higher scores. LLMs such as GPT-4 and Llama 2 have non-trivial accuracy at distinguishing themselves from other LLMs and humans.\n\nBy finetuning LLMs, researchers discovered a linear correlation between self-recognition capability and the strength of self-preference bias; using controlled experiments, they showed that the causal explanation resists straightforward confounders.\n\n## Background and Implications\n\nSelf-evaluation using LLMs has proven valuable in benchmarking and methods like reward modeling, constitutional AI, and self-refinement, but new biases are introduced due to the same LLM acting as both the evaluator and the evaluatee.\n\nThis research appears to have been published in proceedings from NeurIPS 2024 and represents significant work in understanding biases in LLM-based evaluation systems, particularly relevant for AI alignment and safety concerns.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"817b1923-aa16-4c95-bce6-749159f5bef7","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:27:04.675Z","toolUseResult":{"query":"LLM evaluators recognize and favor their own generations self-preference bias measured","results":[{"tool_use_id":"srvtoolu_01MQiKbz8GFF7VtxSsJQUqqL","content":[{"title":"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations","url":"https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/7f1f0218e45f5414c79c0679633e47bc-Abstract-Conference.html"},{"title":"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations | OpenReview","url":"https://openreview.net/forum?id=4NJBV6Wp0h"},{"title":"Quantifying and Mitigating Self-Preference Bias of LLM Judges","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.22891"},{"title":"NeurIPS Poster LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations","url":"https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/poster/96672"},{"title":"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations - Here's what the search results show:\n\n## Key Findings\n\nSelf-preference is a bias where an LLM evaluator scores its own outputs higher than others' while human annotators consider them of equal quality.\n\nThe research investigates whether LLMs actually recognize their own outputs when giving them higher scores. LLMs such as GPT-4 and Llama 2 have non-trivial accuracy at distinguishing themselves from other LLMs and humans.\n\nBy finetuning LLMs, researchers discovered a linear correlation between self-recognition capability and the strength of self-preference bias; using controlled experiments, they showed that the causal explanation resists straightforward confounders.\n\n## Background and Implications\n\nSelf-evaluation using LLMs has proven valuable in benchmarking and methods like reward modeling, constitutional AI, and self-refinement, but new biases are introduced due to the same LLM acting as both the evaluator and the evaluatee.\n\nThis research appears to have been published in proceedings from NeurIPS 2024 and represents significant work in understanding biases in LLM-based evaluation systems, particularly relevant for AI alignment and safety concerns."],"durationSeconds":4.929824317000806,"searchCount":1},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"427ab176-3424-4345-a8c1-e968f21dc1cb","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

workflow\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.05139\"}]\n\nBased on the search results, I found several highly relevant papers on error propagation and failure modes in multi-agent LLM pipelines:\n\n## Key Research Papers Found\n\n**Most Directly Relevant:**\n\n1. **\"The Hallucination Snowball: Modeling Error Propagation as State Transitions in Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines\"** (arXiv:2608.14588)\n   - Presents a formally characterized, empirically measured, and theoretically modeled failure mode in sequential multi-agent LLM pipelines\n   - GPT-4o detection degrades 21.1 percentage points across four stages, with 23.7% of hallucinations escaping entirely, and even the strongest model facing a structural ceiling of 87.0% at best-case conditions\n   - Boundary gates achieve a 42.2 percentage point reduction in hallucination survival\n\n2. **\"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\"** (Cemri et al., arXiv:2503.13657)\n   - Developed from 150 expert-annotated execution traces and scaled to a 1,642-trace dataset across seven frameworks\n\n3. **\"From Spark to Fire: Modeling and Mitigating Error Cascades in LLM-Based Multi-Agent Collaboration\"** (arXiv:2603.04474)\n   - Evaluated six mainstream multi-agent frameworks across three benchmark types, finding that injecting a single error seed into a multi-agent dependency chain leads to widespread failure, with topological fragility impact factors of 6.29 to 10.31\n\n4. **\"Traceability and Accountability in Role-Specialized Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines\"** (arXiv:2510.07614)\n   - Analysis reveals that the vast majority of unrecoverable pipeline failures originate from a flawed plan created in the very first step\n\nThese papers directly address error propagation, per-step failure measurement, and compounding effects in multi-agent LLM pipelines.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown {"parentUuid":"5ca4ca33-94b2-4576-9cff-11746b0e2343","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_019yW1Laa3Wvepcky6mdwSkz","type":"tool_result","content":"# Analysis of arXiv:2603.04474\n\n## Abstract (Verbatim)\n\"Large Language Model-based Multi-Agent Systems (LLM-MAS) are increasingly applied to complex collaborative scenarios. However, their collaborative mechanisms may cause minor inaccuracies to gradually solidify into system-level false consensus through iteration. Such risks are difficult to trace since errors can propagate and amplify through message dependencies. Existing protections often rely on single-agent validation or require modifications to the collaboration architecture, which can weaken effective information flow and may not align with natural collaboration processes in real tasks. To address this, we propose a propagation dynamics model tailored for LLM-MAS that abstracts collaboration as a directed dependency graph and provides an early-stage risk criterion to characterize amplification risk. Through experiments on six mainstream frameworks, we identify three vulnerability classes: cascade amplification, topological sensitivity, and consensus inertia. We further instantiate an attack where injecting just a single atomic error seed leads to widespread failure. In response, we introduce a genealogy-graph-based governance layer, implemented as a message-layer plugin, that suppresses both endogenous and exogenous error amplification without altering the collaboration architecture. Experiments show that this approach prevents final infection in at least 89% of runs across operating modes and significantly mitigates the cascading spread of minor errors.\"\n\n## Venue & Year\nComputer Science > Multiagent Systems; submitted March 4, 2026\n\n## Measured Numbers\n- **Frameworks evaluated:** 6 mainstream multi-agent collaboration systems\n- **Vulnerability classes identified:** 3 (cascade amplification, topological sensitivity, consensus inertia)\n- **Mitigation success rate:** At least 89% prevention of error propagation across operating modes\n- **Single error injection impact:** One atomic error seed causes widespread failure\n\n**Note:** The topological fragility impact factors (6.29–10.31) you referenced are not present in this webpage excerpt. Such risks are difficult to trace since errors can propagate and amplify through message dependencies. Existing protections often rely on single-agent validation or require modifications to the collaboration architecture, which can weaken effective information flow and may not align with natural collaboration processes in real tasks. To address this, we propose a propagation dynamics model tailored for LLM-MAS that abstracts collaboration as a directed dependency graph and provides an early-stage risk criterion to characterize amplification risk. Through experiments on six mainstream frameworks, we identify three vulnerability classes: cascade amplification, topological sensitivity, and consensus inertia. We further instantiate an attack where injecting just a single atomic error seed leads to widespread failure. In response, we introduce a genealogy-graph-based governance layer, implemented as a message-layer plugin, that suppresses both endogenous and exogenous error amplification without altering the collaboration architecture. {"parentUuid":"aa1d166f-c697-481b-ba03-07d969d6177b","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0128zrWheiooz9t6F5caCvqC","type":"tool_result","content":"# LLM Critics Help Catch LLM Bugs - Key Metrics\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is fundamentally limited by the capacity of humans to correctly evaluate model output. To improve human evaluation ability and overcome that limitation this work trains \"critic\" models that help humans to more accurately evaluate model-written code. These critics are themselves LLMs trained with RLHF to write natural language feedback highlighting problems in code from real-world assistant tasks. On code containing naturally occurring LLM errors model-written critiques are preferred over human critiques in 63% of cases, and human evaluation finds that models catch more bugs than human contractors paid for code review. We further confirm that our fine-tuned LLM critics can successfully identify hundreds of errors in ChatGPT training data rated as \"flawless\", even though the majority of those tasks are non-code tasks and thus out-of-distribution for the critic model. Critics can have limitations of their own, including hallucinated bugs that could mislead humans into making mistakes they might have otherwise avoided, but human-machine teams of critics and contractors catch similar numbers of bugs to LLM critics while hallucinating less than LLMs alone.\"\n\n**Venue/Year:** arXiv:2407.00215 [cs.SE], submitted June 28, 2024\n\n**Measured Numbers:**\n- Model critiques preferred over human critiques: **63%**\n- The paper identifies model critics catch more bugs than human contractors but does not specify exact percentages for error detection rates or hallucination rates in the abstract provided.\n\nHuman-machine teams achieved parity with LLM-only critics while producing fewer hallucinated issues, though specific percentages aren't quantified in the abstract."}]},"uuid":"2652b95b-e3ba-46bc-90f2-82d882362ca0","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:27:38.129Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":41256,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# LLM Critics Help Catch LLM Bugs - Key Metrics\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is fundamentally limited by the capacity of humans to correctly evaluate model output. To improve human evaluation ability and overcome that limitation this work trains \"critic\" models that help humans to more accurately evaluate model-written code. These critics are themselves LLMs trained with RLHF to write natural language feedback highlighting problems in code from real-world assistant tasks. On code containing naturally occurring LLM errors model-written critiques are preferred over human critiques in 63% of cases, and human evaluation finds that models catch more bugs than human contractors paid for code review. We further confirm that our fine-tuned LLM critics can successfully identify hundreds of errors in ChatGPT training data rated as \"flawless\", even though the majority of those tasks are non-code tasks and thus out-of-distribution for the critic model. Critics can have limitations of their own, including hallucinated bugs that could mislead humans into making mistakes they might have otherwise avoided, but human-machine teams of critics and contractors catch similar numbers of bugs to LLM critics while hallucinating less than LLMs alone.\"\n\n**Venue/Year:** arXiv:2407.00215 [cs.SE], submitted June 28, 2024\n\n**Measured Numbers:**\n- Model critiques preferred over human critiques: **63%**\n- The paper identifies model critics catch more bugs than human contractors but does not specify exact percentages for error detection rates or hallucination rates in the abstract provided.\n\nHuman-machine teams achieved parity with LLM-only critics while producing fewer hallucinated issues, though specific percentages aren't quantified in the abstract.","durationMs":6498,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.00215"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"aa1d166f-c697-481b-ba03-07d969d6177b","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

Here are the key findings:\n\n## Medical AI & Verification\n\nAblation analysis traces improvement primarily to Two-Phase Verification, while multi-agent reasoning contributes most of the accuracy gains in a study on Multi-Agent Reasoning with Consistency Verification for Medical MCQA.\n\n## Multi-Agent Code Verification\n\nMulti-agent beats single-agent by 39.7 percentage points, with diminishing returns of +14.9pp, +13.5pp, and +11.2pp for agents 2, 3, and 4 in the CodeX-Verify system for code verification via information theory.\n\n## UML Diagram Generation with Verifier\n\nIn the NOMAD system, attribute correctness increases substantially by more than 27.3%, demonstrating the verifier's effectiveness in recovering omitted or misclassified attributes.\n\n## Critic Scope in Image-Derived Hypothesis Testing\n\nInterestingly, in VERITAS, extending the critic beyond code execution into planning and interpretation phases degrades combined evidence accuracy (-10.2pp) and verdict (-4.9pp), suggesting that broader critic scope isn't always beneficial.\n\n## GUI Rewards Framework\n\nIn OS-Themis, without the intermediate verification step, the pipeline suffers from systematic bias and reduced reliability, with accuracy dropping by 6.1% and precision decreasing by 15.6%.\n\nThese papers demonstrate that verifier/critic agents in multi-agent pipelines produce measurable accuracy improvements when appropriately scoped, though the optimal design varies by task domain.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using Paper Analysis: Two Calls Beat Five Agents\n\n## Abstract\n\"Multi-agent LLM pipeline systems break down the task among multiple roles for better reasoning, but are benchmarked mainly with large-scale commercial models.\"\n\nThe study compares a five-agent system called Parishad against simpler approaches using Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.\n\n## Venue & Year\narXiv preprint (cs.LG), submitted July 29, 2026\n\n## Key Measured Comparisons\n\n**GSM8K Dataset (500 questions):**\n- Multi-agent (JSON format): 45.0% accuracy\n- Multi-agent (plaintext format): 82.0% accuracy\n- Two-call self-refinement (V1): 86.2% accuracy with \"7.4× lower token usage\"\n- Direct prompting baseline: 75.0% accuracy\n\n**HumanEval Dataset (164 questions):**\n- Direct prompting baseline: 96.3% accuracy\n- Two-call self-refinement (V1): 66.5% accuracy (performance degradation)\n- Task-aware gated redesign (V2): 95.1% accuracy\n\n## Configuration Details\n- **Multi-agent pipeline:** Five roles/agents\n- **Model tested:** Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (local 7B model)\n- **Key finding:** \"Simpler approaches match or outperform multi-agent pipelines for local 7B model
