{"parentUuid":"ff484b00-abd7-4d85-a10b-0d173c6e7f2c","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016i56rv5r59L4kw7Wxc4PHA","type":"tool_result","content":"# VERITAS Paper Details\n\n**Abstract (Verbatim):**\n\"Scientific research based on multimodal clinical data (including medical imaging) requires coordinating clinical, radiological, programming, and biostatistical expertise, a fragmented process that bottlenecks discovery. We present VERITAS (Verifiable Epistemic Reasoning for Image-Derived Hypothesis Testing via Agentic Systems), a clinical co-scientist: a multi-agent system that autonomously tests natural-language hypotheses and produces a fully auditable evidence trail, tracing every conclusion through executable outputs from analysis plan to segmentation masks to statistical code to final verdict. Unlike prior AI-scientist systems, which mainly operate on tabular or text data, VERITAS grounds autonomous discovery directly in medical images. It decomposes the workflow into four phases handled by role-specialized agents, and introduces an epistemic evidence label framework that mechanically classifies outcomes as Supported, Refuted, Underpowered, or Invalid by jointly evaluating significance, effect direction, and study power. This distinction is critical in medical imaging, where non-significant results often reflect insufficient sample size rather than absent effects. We construct a tiered benchmark of 64 hypotheses spanning six complexity levels across cardiac and brain glioma MRI datasets. VERITAS reaches 81.4% verdict accuracy with frontier models and 71.2% with locally-hosted open-weight models (8-30B), outperforming all single-model baselines in both classes. It also produces the highest rate of independently verifiable statistical outputs (86.6%), so even its failures remain diagnosable through artifact inspection. Structured multi-agent decomposition thus substitutes for model scale while preserving the verifiability that scientific discovery demands.\"\n\n**Venue/Year:** arXiv cs.MA (Computer Science > Multiagent Systems); submitted April 13, 2026; revised July 1, 2026\n\n**Ablation Numbers:** The provided webpage content does not contain specific ablation study figures regarding critic scope degradation (-10.2pp or -4.9pp) or verification stage metrics. These details are not present in this abstract page excerpt."}]},"uuid":"7716191d-9783-4561-8e67-946a0a299c95","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:27:56.737Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":43730,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# VERITAS Paper Details\n\n**Abstract (Verbatim):**\n\"Scientific research based on multimodal clinical data (including medical imaging) requires coordinating clinical, radiological, programming, and biostatistical expertise, a fragmented process that bottlenecks discovery. We present VERITAS (Verifiable Epistemic Reasoning for Image-Derived Hypothesis Testing via Agentic Systems), a clinical co-scientist: a multi-agent system that autonomously tests natural-language hypotheses and produces a fully auditable evidence trail, tracing every conclusion through executable outputs from analysis plan to segmentation masks to statistical code to final verdict. Unlike prior AI-scientist systems, which mainly operate on tabular or text data, VERITAS grounds autonomous discovery directly in medical images. It decomposes the workflow into four phases handled by role-specialized agents, and introduces an epistemic evidence label framework that mechanically classifies outcomes as Supported, Refuted, Underpowered, or Invalid by jointly evaluating significance, effect direction, and study power. This distinction is critical in medical imaging, where non-significant results often reflect insufficient sample size rather than absent effects. We construct a tiered benchmark of 64 hypotheses spanning six complexity levels across cardiac and brain glioma MRI datasets. VERITAS reaches 81.4% verdict accuracy with frontier models and 71.2% with locally-hosted open-weight models (8-30B), outperforming all single-model baselines in both classes. It also produces the highest rate of independently verifiable statistical outputs (86.6%), so even its failures remain diagnosable through artifact inspection. {"parentUuid":"8fe0a92d-e74b-4caa-a9af-6e75dbb4d919","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_0179qyCRrxiffrZAZZqVdPvi","type":"tool_result","content":"# Paper Summary: Traceability and Accountability in Role-Specialized Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Sequential multi-agent systems built with large language models (LLMs) can automate complex software tasks, but they are hard to trust because errors quietly pass from one stage to the next. We study a traceable and accountable pipeline, meaning a system with clear roles, structured handoffs, and saved records that let us trace who did what at each step and assign blame when things go wrong. Our setting is a Planner -> Executor -> Critic pipeline. We evaluate eight configurations of three state-of-the-art LLMs on three benchmarks and analyze where errors start, how they spread, and how they can be fixed. Our results show: (1) adding a structured, accountable handoff between agents markedly improves accuracy and prevents the failures common in simple pipelines; (2) models have clear role-specific strengths and risks (e.g., steady planning vs. high-variance critiquing), which we quantify with repair and harm rates; and (3) accuracy-cost-latency trade-offs are task-dependent, with heterogeneous pipelines often the most efficient. Overall, we provide a practical, data-driven method for designing, tracing, and debugging reliable, predictable, and accountable multi-agent systems.\"\n\n**Venue and Year:**\n2025 40th ACM/IEEE International Conference on Automated Software Engineering Workshops\n\n**Measured Failure Numbers:**\nThe provided web page excerpt does not contain specific percentages, per-role failure attribution statistics, or task evaluation counts. These detailed metrics appear in the full paper itself, which is not included in this page content."}]},"uuid":"4a3020fd-1e21-46d3-80e2-3e3ceb803158","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:28:13.318Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":41106,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Paper Summary: Traceability and Accountability in Role-Specialized Multi-Agent LLM Pipelines\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Sequential multi-agent systems built with large language models (LLMs) can automate complex software tasks, but they are hard to trust because errors quietly pass from one stage to the next. We study a traceable and accountable pipeline, meaning a system with clear roles, structured handoffs, and saved records that let us trace who did what at each step and assign blame when things go wrong. Our setting is a Planner -> Executor -> Critic pipeline. We evaluate eight configurations of three state-of-the-art LLMs on three benchmarks and analyze where errors start, how they spread, and how they can be fixed. Our results show: (1) adding a structured, accountable handoff between agents markedly improves accuracy and prevents the failures common in simple pipelines; (2) models have clear role-specific strengths and risks (e.g., steady planning vs. high-variance critiquing), which we quantify with repair and harm rates; and (3) accuracy-cost-latency trade-offs are task-dependent, with heterogeneous pipelines often the most efficient. Overall, we provide a practical, data-driven method for designing, tracing, and debugging reliable, predictable, and accountable multi-agent systems.\"\n\n**Venue and Year:**\n2025 40th ACM/IEEE International Conference on Automated Software Engineering Workshops\n\n**Measured Failure Numbers:**\nThe provided web page excerpt does not contain specific percentages, per-role failure attribution statistics, or task evaluation counts. The exact results show:\n\n**Critic Removal Impact (Local Veritas):**\n- Evidence accuracy: 63.4% (default) → 60.8% (no critic) = **-2.6 percentage points**\n- Verdict accuracy: 71.4% (default) → 73.2% (no critic) = **+1.8 percentage points**\n\nThe paper's analysis notes: \"Critic removal has minimal impact (++1.8pp combined verdict), indicating the iterative code-revision loop already provides sufficient error correction.\"\n\n**Scope Limitation:**\nThe ablation does not explicitly test expanding critic involvement into planning or interpretation phases. The critic operates \"by default in execution phases (2A/2B)\" according to the framework description, but the ablation only measures removal entirely rather than extension into other phases. {"parentUuid":"cdb9baa2-6da9-45cb-a04b-b4be7b8b766d","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01JpwCmcHgPxYGe6tNb4nq7Q","type":"tool_result","content":"# MAS Frameworks and Failure Analysis\n\n## Frameworks Studied\n\nThe research examined seven open-source multi-agent systems:\n\n1. **ChatDev** - Hierarchical workflow simulating software engineering phases\n2. **MetaGPT** - Assembly line architecture simulating company SOPs\n3. **HyperAgent** - Hierarchical workflow with central Planner coordinating specialists\n4. **AppWorld** - Star topology with tool-calling agents\n5. **AG2** - Agentic framework for building agents and managing interactions\n6. **Magentic-One** - Generalist system for complex, open-ended tasks\n7. **OpenManus** - Hierarchical framework for collaborative AI agents\n\n## Failure Rate Range\n\nThe paper states: *\"Our empirical analysis reveals 41% to 86.7% failure rate on 7 state-of-the-art (SOTA) open-source MAS\"*\n\n## Dataset Composition\n\n**MAST-Data** contains **1,642 annotated execution traces** total. Of these, **21 traces** comprise MAST-Data-human, which were annotated by three human experts each during inter-annotator agreement studies. The remaining traces were annotated using the LLM-as-a-Judge pipeline.\n\n## Case Study Results\n\nThe intervention studies showed modest improvements through system design modifications with the same underlying model, but comprehensive success rates weren't fully restored, indicating that more fundamental redesigns are necessary beyond tactical adjustments."}]},"uuid":"4919ea6a-2601-4d9c-8a34-f4d2cbbbd13e","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:28:31.779Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":1781365,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# MAS Frameworks and Failure Analysis\n\n## Frameworks Studied\n\nThe research examined seven open-source multi-agent systems:\n\n1. **ChatDev** - Hierarchical workflow simulating software engineering phases\n2. **MetaGPT** - Assembly line architecture simulating company SOPs\n3. **HyperAgent** - Hierarchical workflow with central Planner coordinating specialists\n4. **AppWorld** - Star topology with tool-calling agents\n5. **AG2** - Agentic framework for building agents and managing interactions\n6. **Magentic-One** - Generalist system for complex, open-ended tasks\n7. **OpenManus** - Hierarchical framework for collaborative AI agents\n\n## Failure Rate Range\n\nThe paper states: *\"Our empirical analysis reveals 41% to 86.7% failure rate on 7 state-of-the-art (SOTA) open-source MAS\"*\n\n## Dataset Composition\n\n**MAST-Data** contains **1,642 annotated execution traces** total. Of these, **21 traces** comprise MAST-Data-human, which were annotated by three human experts each during inter-annotator agreement studies. The remaining traces were annotated using the LLM-as-a-Judge pipeline.\n\n## Case Study Results\n\nThe intervention studies showed modest improvements through system design modifications with the same underlying model, but comprehensive success rates weren't fully restored, indicating that more fundamental redesigns are necessary beyond tactical adjustments.","durationMs":5985,"url":"https://arxiv.org/html/2503.13657v3"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"cdb9baa2-6da9-45cb-a04b-b4be7b8b766d","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

{"parentUuid":"305e0c11-dc71-418b-990d-8f4d233beb17","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01CK4UWsMVkcNwNezcxHCPiP","type":"tool_result","content":"Web search results for query: \"multi-agent LLM system latency measured wall-clock scaling number of agents orchestration overhead benchmark\"\n\nLinks: [{\"title\":\"Learning Latency-Aware Orchestration for Multi-Agent Systems\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2601.10560\"},{\"title\":\"Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Architectures for Financial Document Processing: A Comparative Study of Orchestration Patterns, Cost-Accuracy Tradeoffs and Production Scaling Strategies\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2603.22651v1\"},{\"title\":\"Agent Memory Below the Prompt: Persistent Q4 KV Cache for Multi-Agent LLM Inference on Edge Devices\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04428v1\"},{\"title\":\"LLM Speed Benchmarks: Metrics & Infrastructure Guide\",\"url\":\"https://redis.io/blog/llm-speed-benchmarks/\"},{\"title\":\"Beyond GeneGPT: A Multi-Agent Architecture with Open-Source LLMs for Enhanced Genomic Question Answering\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.15061\"},{\"title\":\"Latency and Cost of Multi-Agent Intelligent Tutoring at Scale\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.24110\"},{\"title\":\"Understanding Multi-Agent LLM Frameworks: A Unified Benchmark and Experimental Analysis\",\"url\":\"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2602.03128\"}]\n\nI found several relevant research papers and resources on multi-agent LLM systems with latency and orchestration measurements:\n\n## Key Findings:\n\n**Latency Measurement & Wall-Clock Scaling:**\n\nCP Tokens are strongly correlated with measured wall-clock latency in multi-agent systems, with LAMaS achieving the lowest latency across all benchmarks. Latency metrics include wall-clock time from document submission to structured output measured at the system level.\n\n**Orchestration Overhead & Architecture Impact:**\n\nArchitectural choices alone can induce order-of-magnitude differences in latency and throughput, requiring evaluation of orchestration overhead, memory behavior, planning, specialization, and coordination. Framework-level design choices can increase latency by over 100×, reduce planning accuracy by up to 30%, and lower coordination success from above 90% to below 30%.\n\n**Multi-Agent Scaling Patterns:**\n\nDifferent multi-agent orchestration patterns (sequential, parallel, hierarchical, reflexive) are evaluated on extraction accuracy, latency and cost, with performance characteristics changing as processing volume scales from 1K to 100K documents per day.\n\n**Benchmarking Frameworks:**\n\nKey benchmarks mentioned include MAFBench, a unified evaluation suite that integrates existing benchmarks under a standardized execution pipeline, and a rigorous benchmark framework for evaluating multi-agent LLM architectures with multiple models and four architectures yielding 500 distinct experimental configurations.\n\nThese resources provide comprehensive measurements of latency scaling with number of agents and quantify orchestration overhead in production multi-agent systems.\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks."}]},"uuid":"29aa5f77-d13d-4d2e-8089-9877f0c7e184","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:28:48.592Z","toolUseResult":{"query":"multi-agent LLM system latency measured wall-clock scaling number of {"parentUuid":"c52e4582-d1a9-4c48-96c9-cddf6af72d65","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01HakgdtF2N1f9EKd9obZt7u","type":"tool_result","content":"# Research Paper Summary\n\n**Venue & Year:** ACM Conference on AI and Agentic Systems (2026)\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Multi-agent debate, where teams of LLMs iteratively exchange rationales and vote on answers, is widely deployed under the assumption that peer review filters hallucinations. Yet the failure dynamics of homogeneous debate remain poorly understood, therefore we report findings from a controlled empirical study of teams of N=10 homogeneous agents (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Ministral-3-8B) across R=3 debate rounds on two high-difficulty benchmarks (GSM-Hard and MMLU-Hard)...\"\n\n**Key Measured Numbers:**\n\n- **Token Cost Multiplier:** Debate consumed \"2.1-3.4× more tokens (up to 28,631 tokens per problem)\" compared to isolated self-correction\n- **Accuracy:** Debate yielded \"equal or lower accuracy\" than self-correction despite higher token consumption\n- **Failure Mechanisms:**\n  - Sycophantic conformity: modal adoption up to 85.5%\n  - Contextual fragility: vulnerability rate up to 70.0%\n  - Consensus collapse: oracle gap up to 32.3 percentage points\n\n**Experimental Configuration:**\n- **Agents:** N = 10 homogeneous models (three model types tested)\n- **Debate Rounds:** R = 3\n- **Benchmarks:** GSM-Hard and MMLU-Hard\n- **Communication Density (K):** {2, 4, 9}\n- **Sampling Temperature (T):** {0.4, 0.7}"}]},"uuid":"235454bd-5228-4f57-b9c2-34b2f50197e2","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:29:09.601Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":44398,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# Research Paper Summary\n\n**Venue & Year:** ACM Conference on AI and Agentic Systems (2026)\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Multi-agent debate, where teams of LLMs iteratively exchange rationales and vote on answers, is widely deployed under the assumption that peer review filters hallucinations. Yet the failure dynamics of homogeneous debate remain poorly understood, therefore we report findings from a controlled empirical study of teams of N=10 homogeneous agents (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Ministral-3-8B) across R=3 debate rounds on two high-difficulty benchmarks (GSM-Hard and MMLU-Hard)...\"\n\n**Key Measured Numbers:**\n\n- **Token Cost Multiplier:** Debate consumed \"2.1-3.4× more tokens (up to 28,631 tokens per problem)\" compared to isolated self-correction\n- **Accuracy:** Debate yielded \"equal or lower accuracy\" than self-correction despite higher token consumption\n- **Failure Mechanisms:**\n  - Sycophantic conformity: modal adoption up to 85.5%\n  - Contextual fragility: vulnerability rate up to 70.0%\n  - Consensus collapse: oracle gap up to 32.3 percentage points\n\n**Experimental Configuration:**\n- **Agents:** N = 10 homogeneous models (three model types tested)\n- **Debate Rounds:** R = 3\n- **Benchmarks:** GSM-Hard and MMLU-Hard\n- **Communication Density (K):** {2, 4, 9}\n- **Sampling Temperature (T):** {0.4, 0.7}","durationMs":6437,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"c52e4582-d1a9-4c48-96c9-cddf6af72d65","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

Compressed contexts may preserve the general topic while dropping essential details, motivating grounding and information-preservation metrics beyond efficiency.\n\n**The Challenge of Summarization in Multi-Agent Systems:**\n\nContext compaction via LLM-based summarization keeps conversation bounded, but summarization is inherently lossy and the blocking call stalls agent inference. Additionally, the operator has no fine-grained control over summary volume since prompt instructions are largely ignored, and as context grows, both the amount of output tokens the model produces and the information it retains fluctuate substantially from run to run.\n\n**Notable Research Frameworks:**\n\nSeveral recent arXiv papers address this issue:\n- ACON (Agent Context Optimization) optimally compresses histories and observations into concise summaries, reducing peak tokens and memory\n- Focus addresses \"Context Bloat\" where computational costs explode, latency increases, and reasoning capabilities degrade due to distraction (arXiv 2601.07190)\n- Parallel Context Compaction for Long-Horizon LLM Agent Serving characterizes compression approaches across multiple model backbones (arXiv 2605.23296)\n\n\nREMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown Compressed contexts may preserve the general topic while dropping essential details, motivating grounding and information-preservation metrics beyond efficiency.\n\n**The Challenge of Summarization in Multi-Agent Systems:**\n\nContext compaction via LLM-based summarization keeps conversation bounded, but summarization is inherently lossy and the blocking call stalls agent inference. Additionally, the operator has no fine-grained control over summary volume since prompt instructions are largely ignored, and as context grows, both the amount of output tokens the model produces and the information it retains fluctuate substantially from run to run.\n\n**Notable Research Frameworks:**\n\nSeveral recent arXiv papers address this issue:\n- ACON (Agent Context Optimization) optimally compresses histories and observations into concise summaries, reducing peak tokens and memory\n- Focus addresses \"Context Bloat\" where computational costs explode, latency increases, and reasoning capabilities degrade due to distraction (arXiv 2601.07190)\n- Parallel Context Compaction for Long-Horizon LLM Agent Serving characterizes compression approaches across multiple model backbones (arXiv 2605.23296)"],"durationSeconds":6.951915734000504,"searchCount":1},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"b687bed6-8cf8-42f7-8dd7-cb74adc095f4","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

{"parentUuid":"809eff4d-8a97-4254-b8c5-09fd187cbc38","isSidechain":true,"promptId":"4b1d3127-55f1-48c9-8730-577869d23440","agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01JBqcxeKvDpUeStnMgujRxX","type":"tool_result","content":"# ACON Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents in dynamic real-world environments, where success depends on maintaining precise records of actions and observations. However, the resulting unbounded context growth in long-horizon agentic tasks makes two critical bottlenecks: prohibitive inference memory costs and reasoning degradation due to irrelevant information. Existing compression methods fail to fully address this, often relying on brittle heuristics or requiring parameter updates impractical for proprietary or large-scale LLMs. We introduce Agent Context Optimization (ACON), a unified framework that optimally compresses both observations and history into concise, informative representations. Distinct from prior works, ACON employs an optimization in natural language space: it iteratively refines compression guidelines based on failure analysis of the agent, ensuring critical state information is preserved without model fine-tuning. To further minimize computational overhead, we distill the optimized compressor into smaller models. Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA demonstrate that ACON reduces peak token usage by 26-54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines. Notably, it enables smaller LMs to function effectively as long-horizon agents, achieving up to 46% performance improvement by mitigating context distraction. Our code is available at [this https URL](https://github.com/microsoft/acon).\"\n\n**Venue & Year:** ICML 2026\n\n**Key Metrics:**\n- Peak token usage reduction: 26-54%\n- Performance improvement for smaller LMs: up to 46%\n\n**Benchmarks:** AppWorld, OfficeBench, Multi-objective QA\n\n**Note:** The abstract does not provide specific performance degradation numbers for naive context compression approaches."}]},"uuid":"972f5b5e-0ec8-4622-b6dc-29b2c89306a6","timestamp":"2026-08-21T04:29:34.818Z","toolUseResult":{"bytes":43368,"code":200,"codeText":"OK","result":"# ACON Paper Summary\n\n**Abstract (verbatim):**\n\"Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents in dynamic real-world environments, where success depends on maintaining precise records of actions and observations. However, the resulting unbounded context growth in long-horizon agentic tasks makes two critical bottlenecks: prohibitive inference memory costs and reasoning degradation due to irrelevant information. Existing compression methods fail to fully address this, often relying on brittle heuristics or requiring parameter updates impractical for proprietary or large-scale LLMs. We introduce Agent Context Optimization (ACON), a unified framework that optimally compresses both observations and history into concise, informative representations. Distinct from prior works, ACON employs an optimization in natural language space: it iteratively refines compression guidelines based on failure analysis of the agent, ensuring critical state information is preserved without model fine-tuning. To further minimize computational overhead, we distill the optimized compressor into smaller models. Experiments on AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Multi-objective QA demonstrate that ACON reduces peak token usage by 26-54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines. Notably, it enables smaller LMs to function effectively as long-horizon agents, achieving up to 46% performance improvement by mitigating context distraction. Our code is available at [this https URL](https://github.com/microsoft/acon).\"\n\n**Venue & Year:** ICML 2026\n\n**Key Metrics:**\n- Peak token usage reduction: 26-54%\n- Performance improvement for smaller LMs: up to 46%\n\n**Benchmarks:** AppWorld, OfficeBench, Multi-objective QA\n\n**Note:** The abstract does not provide specific performance degradation numbers for naive context compression approaches.","durationMs":6560,"url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00615"},"sourceToolAssistantUUID":"809eff4d-8a97-4254-b8c5-09fd187cbc38","userType":"external","entrypoint":"cli","cwd":"/private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-chidionyema-Documents-code-prospector/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}

r/3fa47c70-c6d2-4273-9620-19dc9810b132/scratchpad/wt-storeroot","sessionId":"0d5d261b-d4bc-4fc1-a680-9c2b5af6a13c","version":"2.1.238","gitBranch":"HEAD","slug":"splendid-percolating-engelbart"}
{"parentUuid":"f4c01c2e-1a10-42d3-891c-7170be0c0c4c","isSidechain":true,"agentId":"a2c9082538fa5876b","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CeFGMYDUAc4CStAyxrmHC","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"text","text":"DONE: 40 sourced measurements across the five areas. Two things to flag up front — the MAST percentages differ between arXiv versions (v1/v2 vs v3), and the strongest anti-multi-agent evidence is all matched-compute, which is the comparison most papers do not run.\n\n---\n\n# FINDINGS\n\n## 1. MAST — the actual numbers\n\nSource for all of §1: Cemri, Pan, Yang et al., **\"Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?\"**, NeurIPS 2025 Datasets & Benchmarks, [arXiv:2503.13657](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657).\n\n**Scope.** 1,642 annotated execution traces (MAST-Data) across 7 open-source MAS frameworks: ChatDev, MetaGPT, HyperAgent, AppWorld, AG2, Magentic-One, OpenManus. Taxonomy built from 150 traces. Human inter-annotator agreement **κ = 0.88**. LLM-as-a-Judge annotator: **94% accuracy, Cohen's κ = 0.77** with few-shot; **κ = 0.79** generalising to unseen systems.\n\n**Headline failure rate: \"41% to 86.7% failure rate on 7 state-of-the-art (SOTA) open-source MAS\"** (verbatim, v2/v3).\n\n**The 14 failure modes.** The percentages MOVED between versions. Both sets below are real; v3 is current.\n\n| | Failure mode | v3 (1,600+ traces) | v2 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| **FC1 System design** | | **43.8%** | **41.77%** |\n| FM-1.1 | Disobey task specification | 11.8% | 10.98% |\n| FM-1.2 | Disobey role specification | 1.5% | — |\n| FM-1.3 | Step repetition | 15.7% | 17.14% |\n| FM-1.4 | Loss of conversation history | 2.80% | — |\n| FM-1.5 | Unaware of termination conditions | 12.4% | — |\n| **FC2 Inter-agent misalignment** | | **32.35%** | **36.94%** |\n| FM-2.1 | Conversation reset | 2.20% | — |\n| FM-2.2 | Fail to ask for clarification | 6.80% | 11.65% |\n| FM-2.3 | Task derailment | 7.40% | — |\n| FM-2.4 | Information withholding | 0.85% | — |\n| FM-2.5 | Ignored other agent's input | 1.90% | — |\n| FM-2.6 | Reasoning-action mismatch | 13.2% | 13.98% |\n| **FC3 Task verification** | | **23.5%** | **21.30%** |\n| FM-3.1 | Premature termination | 6.20% | — |\n| FM-3.2 | No or incomplete verification | 8.20% | — |\n| FM-3.3 | Incorrect verification | 9.10% | — |\n\n**MAST's own interventions** (numbers also differ by version — v1 via subagent, v2/v3 via my fetch):\n- ChatDev ProgramDev: **25.0% → 40.6%, +15.6pp**, from adding high-level task-objective verification / topology change. (v3 fetch reports the same +15.6% against a 33.33% baseline — the baseline moved, the delta did not.)\n- ChatDev HumanEval: 89.6% → 91.5% (+1.9pp).\n- AG2: **+9.4%** on MathChat from making the CEO role final arbiter (v2/v3); v1 reports AG2 on GSM-Plus GPT-4 84.75% → 89.75% (+5.0pp) from an improved prompt.\n- Verbatim: *\"the gain in accuracy or performance remains minimal compared to single agent frameworks or even simple baselines such as best-of-N sampling.\"*\n\n**Implication:** the three biggest single modes are step repetition (15.7%), reasoning-action mismatch (13.2%), and unaware of termination (12.4%). A divide-and-conquer protocol needs an explicit, machine-checkable done-condition and a repetition detector before it needs better prompts. Verification is only ~23% of failures, so a verifier stage alone caps out at about a quarter of the problem.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Multi-agent vs single-agent on the same task\n\n### Where multi-agent LOSES\n\n**2.1 — Debate loses to self-consistency at matched response count.** GSM8K, GPT-3.5: debate @6 responses **83.2%** vs self-consistency @6 **85.3%**; debate @9 **83.0%** vs self-consistency @9 **88.2%**. Verbatim: *\"multi-agent debate significantly underperforms simple self-consistency using majority voting.\"* Huang et al., **\"Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet\"**, ICLR 2024, [arXiv:2310.01798](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798), Table 7. → Benchmark any protocol against N independent samples + vote at the same N. The gap grows with N (−2.1pp at 6, −5.2pp at 9).\n\n**2.2 — Single agent beats sequential multi-agent at equal thinking-token budget, in every configuration tested.** FRAMES and MuSiQue 4-hop, budgets 1k and 5k. Single-agent minus multi-agent accuracy ranges **+0.008 to +0.096, never negative**, across Qwen3-30B, DeepSeek-R1-70B, Gemini 2.5 Flash/Pro. Tran & Kiela (Stanford), [arXiv:2604.02460](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02460), 2026. → Splitting context costs more than decomposition buys on dependent reasoning; the gap widens with weaker workers.\n\n**2.3 — The multi-agent gain disappears once the single-agent prompt is good.** FOLIO-wiki, ChatGPT-3.5, with demonstrations: single agent **77.39%** = best discussion framework CMD (6 agents) **77.39%**, both above Debate 75.65% and MAD 74.13%. All-task average with demos: single **75.63%** > CMD 74.46%. Without demos the ordering flips (single 67.41% < CMD 70.75%). Wang et al., **\"Rethinking the Bounds of LLM Reasoning\"**, ACL 2024, [arXiv:2402.18272](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.18272). → Multi-agent is partly a substitute for a good prompt. Fix the prompt, then re-measure.\n\n**2.4 — Homogeneous debate loses to isolated self-correction on every model × benchmark tested, at 2.1–3.4× the tokens.** 10 agents, 3 rounds. GSM-Hard: Qwen2.5-7B 58.8% vs **61.0%**; Ministral-3-8B 20.7% vs **48.3%** (−27.6pp); Llama-3.1-8B 39.3% vs **41.0%**. MMLU-Hard: −6.0, −3.3, −2.7pp. Tokens 18,240 vs 5,396. Mechanisms: modal-answer adoption up to **85.5%**, correct-answer destabilisation up to **70.0%**, and an **oracle gap up to 32.3pp** — the correct answer was in the pool and consensus discarded it. Bertalanič & Fortuna, **\"The Cost of Consensus\"**, ACM Conf. on AI and Agentic Systems 2026, [arXiv:2605.00914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914). → Use a verifier to SELECT, never a consensus vote.\n\n**2.5 — Two calls beat five agents on a local 7B model.** GSM8K (500 q), Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct: five-agent pipeline 82.0% (plaintext) / 45.0% (JSON) vs two-call self-refinement **86.2% at 7.4× lower token usage**; direct prompting 75.0%. HumanEval: direct prompting 96.3% beat both. [arXiv:2607.26922](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26922), 2026. → The JSON-vs-plaintext 37pp swing is itself a finding: output format is a bigger lever than agent count.\n\n**2.6 — A fixed non-agentic pipeline beat every open-source agent on SWE-bench Lite.** Agentless (localize → repair → validate, no tool use, no agent-chosen actions): **32.00%** resolve, **$0.70/issue**. Xia et al., **\"Agentless\"**, [arXiv:2407.01489](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.01489). → On coding, a decomposition YOU designed beat letting agents choose one.\n\n**2.7 — Systematic 5 × 9 × 4 sweep: MAD fails to reliably beat CoT or self-consistency while spending more compute.** [arXiv:2502.08788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788) (abstract-level claim only; see GAPS).\n\n**2.8 — Cognition's \"Don't Build Multi-Agents\" contains no measurements at all.** Zero benchmarks, zero evals; one Flappy Bird anecdote about subagents working from *\"conflicting assumptions not prescribed upfront\"*. [cognition.com/blog/dont-build-multi-agents](https://cognition.com/blog/dont-build-multi-agents), June 2025. → Cite as a design argument, never as evidence. Its mechanism claim is independently confirmed by 2.9 below.\n\n### Where multi-agent WINS\n\n**2.9 — The task-structure result, and the most usable one.** 260 configurations (v1: 180), 6 agentic benchmarks, 5 architectures, 3 LLM families, **standardised compute**. Relative change vs single-agent baseline spans **+80.8% on decomposable financial reasoning to −70.0% on sequential planning**. PlanCraft: Centralized −50.4%, Independent −70.0%. Finance Agent: Centralized +80.9%. Error amplification: independent agents **17.2×** vs single-agent, centralized coordination **4.4×**. Verbatim findings: *\"coordination yields diminishing returns once single-agent baselines exceed certain performance\"* (v1 gives an empirical threshold of **~45%**); *\"tool-heavy tasks appear to incur multi-agent overhead\"*; *\"architectures without centralized verification tend to propagate errors more.\"* Cross-validated **R² = 0.373** (v1: 0.513). Kim et al. (Google Research / MIT / Google DeepMind), **\"Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems\"**, [arXiv:2512.08296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296). → Two mechanical gates worth encoding: do not split tool-heavy sequential work; do not split when single-agent is already above ~45%. And route through a central coordinator — 17.2× vs 4.4× error amplification.\n\n**2.10 — Anthropic: +90.2% over single-agent, at ~15× tokens.** *\"multi-agent system with Claude Opus 4 as the lead agent and Claude Sonnet 4 subagents outperformed single-agent Claude Opus 4 by 90.2%\"*; *\"agents typically use about 4× more tokens than chat interactions, and multi-agent systems use about 15× more tokens as chats\"*; on BrowseComp *\"token usage by itself explains 80% of the variance\"*, three factors together 95%. Parallelisation *\"cut research time by up to 90% for complex queries\"*. Their own exclusion: *\"most coding tasks involve fewer truly parallelizable tasks than research.\"* [anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/multi-agent-research-system), June 2025. → **Not matched-compute.** 15× tokens against a 1× baseline, and their own finding is that 80% of variance is token spend. Much of the 90.2% may be budget, not topology.\n\n**2.11 — Multi-agent wins at HIGH compute budgets, where self-consistency has already saturated.** MMLU-Pro and BBH at equal budget: debate **+1.3pp** over self-consistency, mixture-of-agents **+2.7pp**; up to **+7.1pp** over CoT at 20× CoT compute. Design rule verbatim: *\"mixture-of-agents is most efficient when the number of parallel generations exceeds the number of sequential aggregations.\"* Wunderlich et al., SRW @ ACL 2026, [arXiv:2605.01566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01566). → Reconciles with 2.1: self-consistency plateaus, so multi-agent only wins above the plateau. Wide parallel, shallow aggregation.\n\n**2.12 — Sampling-and-voting scales with agent count, gains concentrated on weak models.** Ensemble 40 vs 1: GSM8K Llama2-13B 0.35→0.59, GPT-3.5 0.73→0.85; HumanEval Llama2-13B 0.14→0.25 (+79% rel.) vs GPT-3.5 0.67→0.73 (+9% rel.). Li et al., **\"More Agents Is All You Need\"**, TMLR 2024, [arXiv:2402.05120](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.05120). → Ensembling, not division of labour: no agent does a different job. Consistent with 2.9's capability ceiling.\n\n**2.13 — Scaffold choice moves accuracy more than model choice, and does not transfer across model families.** GAIA, 3 scaffolds × 5 models: *\"scaffold choice alone moves measured accuracy by as much as 28 percentage points within a single model.\"* The multi-agent advantage appeared *\"within the Anthropic family but not for the cross-provider models.\"* Cheapest and most accurate at Level 2 was the simplest non-ReAct scaffold. [arXiv:2606.08529](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08529), 2026. → A protocol validated on one model family is not evidence for another.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Coordination overhead — how cost and latency scale\n\n**3.1 — Token cost grows 36–49× going from 1 to 8 agents.** Verbatim: *\"under 4 rounds, as the number of agents increases from 1 to 8, the token cost in GSM8K/Arithmetic/MMLU has respectively grown by 36×/44×/49×\"*, and *\"under 4 agents, as the number of rounds increases from 1 to 4, the token cost ... increased by 17×/29×/19×.\"* GroupDebate's grouped topology cuts tokens **13.6–30.6%** per dataset (up to 51.7% claimed) with accuracy changes of **+5.2pp (Arithmetic), +6.1pp (MMLU), −0.4pp (GSM8K), −1.2pp (MATH), +1.4pp (GPQA)**. Liu et al., **\"GroupDebate\"**, AAMAS 2026, [arXiv:2409.14051](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14051). → Cost is superlinear in agent count (all-to-all messaging) while accuracy is not. Sparsify the communication graph.\n\n**3.2 — 2.1–3.4× token multiplier for debate, up to 28,631 tokens per problem, for equal or worse accuracy.** [arXiv:2605.00914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914) (see 2.4).\n\n**3.3 — Framework-level design choices alone move latency by over 100×.** Verbatim from the abstract: framework choices *\"increase latency by over 100×, reduce planning accuracy by up to 30%, and lower coordination success from above 90% to below 30%.\"* MAFBench, **\"Understanding Multi-Agent LLM Frameworks: A Unified Benchmark and Experimental Analysis\"**, [arXiv:2602.03128](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03128), 2026. → Abstract-level only; per-framework table not extracted.\n\n**3.4 — Anthropic's operational numbers**: 4× tokens for single agents vs chat, 15× for multi-agent; research time cut up to 90% by parallelisation; a single improved tool description gave a *\"40% decrease in task completion time.\"* → Latency and cost move in OPPOSITE directions under parallel fan-out: wall-clock down, tokens up. Same source as 2.10.\n\n**3.5 — Diminishing then negative returns are the general shape.** Non-monotone in LLM calls: *\"the performance of both Vote and Filter-Vote can first increase but then decrease as a function of the number of LM calls\"* — easy queries benefit from more votes, hard queries are hurt. Chen et al., **\"Are More LLM Calls All You Need?\"**, NeurIPS 2024, [arXiv:2403.02419](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.02419). → Fan-out width has an optimum, not a monotone gain. Tune per task difficulty.\n\n---\n\n## 4. Spec drift, information loss, inter-agent misalignment\n\n**4.1 — 39% average performance drop from single-turn to multi-turn on the same underspecified task.** 200,000+ simulated conversations, six generation tasks, all top open- and closed-weight LLMs. Decomposition: *\"a minor loss in aptitude and a significant increase in unreliability.\"* Verbatim finding: *\"when LLMs take a wrong turn in a conversation, they get lost and do not recover.\"* Laban, Hayashi, Zhou, Neville (Microsoft Research / Salesforce), **\"LLMs Get Lost In Multi-Turn Conversation\"**, [arXiv:2505.06120](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06120), 2025. → A parent that dribbles a spec out to a child across turns loses ~39% versus handing over the fully-specified task once. Give children complete specs up front.\n\n**4.2 — Problem drift measured directly in multi-agent debate.** Drift occurs in **76–89% of generative tasks** and 7–21% of high-complexity tasks. Root causes over 170 analysed debates: **lack of progress 35%, low-quality feedback 26%, lack of clarity 25%**. Their DRIFTPolicy mitigation addresses **31%** of drift cases. Becker et al., **\"Stay Focused: Problem Drift in Multi-Agent Debate\"**, EACL 2026, [arXiv:2502.19559](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19559). → Drift is the norm on open-ended work, not the exception, and a mitigation that catches a third of it is state of the art.\n\n**4.3 — Hallucination survival compounds across handoffs, and per-boundary escape rises sharply.** 4-stage pipeline, 346 injected hallucinations, FinanceBench. GPT-4o detection degrades **72.0% → 50.9%** from stage 1 to stage 4 (−21.1pp). Per-boundary escape probabilities **24.6%, 48.3%, 89.3%**. **23.7%** of hallucinations reach the final output undetected. Catchable at the first boundary: 75.4%; escaped by the third: 89.3%. Even the strongest model tested tops out at **87.0%** stage-1 detection. **Boundary gates cut hallucination survival to 16.2% vs 58.4% for end-checking** (a 42.2pp reduction, Cohen's h = −0.911, p < 0.000001); end-checking alone improves only **2.3pp**. **\"The Hallucination Snowball\"**, FAGEN Workshop @ ICML 2026, [arXiv:2608.14588](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14588). → **The single most actionable finding for a divide-and-conquer protocol: verify at every handoff, not at the end.** A final review stage buys 2.3pp; per-boundary gates buy 42.2pp.\n\n**4.4 — A single injected error cascades through the dependency graph.** Six mainstream MAS frameworks. Verbatim: *\"injecting just a single atomic error seed leads to widespread failure\"*, via three vulnerability classes — cascade amplification, topological sensitivity, consensus inertia. A message-layer governance plugin *\"prevents final infection in at least 89% of runs.\"* **\"From Spark to Fire\"**, [arXiv:2603.04474](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04474), 2026.\n\n**4.5 — Failures originate at the plan, and structured handoffs are what fix them.** Planner → Executor → Critic pipeline, 8 configurations of 3 LLMs, 3 benchmarks. Verbatim: *\"adding a structured, accountable handoff between agents markedly improves accuracy and prevents the failures common in simple pipelines\"*; roles have distinct risk profiles (*\"steady planning vs. high-variance critiquing\"*), quantified as repair and harm rates. ASE Workshops 2025, [arXiv:2510.07614](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07614). → The handoff FORMAT is a lever independent of model quality.\n\n**4.6 — Context compression at handoff is lossy in a measurable way.** ACON reduces peak token usage 26–54% while improving task success over existing compression baselines, and gives small LMs **up to 46%** improvement by mitigating context distraction — i.e. naive compression was leaving that much on the table. AppWorld, OfficeBench, Multi-objective QA. Kang et al. (Microsoft), **ACON**, ICML 2026, [arXiv:2510.00615](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00615). → Compression guidelines have to be optimised against observed failures, not written by hand.\n\n**4.7 — MAST's own inter-agent misalignment class: 32.35%**, of which reasoning-action mismatch is 13.2%, task derailment 7.4%, fail-to-ask-for-clarification 6.8%, ignored-other-agent's-input 1.9%, information withholding 0.85%. See §1. → Note how SMALL deliberate information withholding is (0.85%). The loss is not agents hiding things; it is agents acting inconsistently with their own stated reasoning (13.2%) and never asking (6.8%).\n\n---\n\n## 5. Verification and review stages\n\n### Verification HELPS\n\n**5.1 — A trained verifier beats majority voting by 8.6pp at best-of-1860.** MATH test subset: process-supervised reward model **78.2%**, outcome-supervised RM **72.4%**, majority voting **69.6%**. Verbatim: *\"While the ORM performs slightly better than the majority voting baseline, the PRM strongly outperforms both\"*, and the PRM–ORM gap *\"widens as N increases.\"* Lightman et al. (OpenAI), **\"Let's Verify Step by Step\"**, ICLR 2024, [arXiv:2305.20050](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.20050). → Selection by a verifier beats selection by vote, and step-level feedback beats final-answer feedback. This is the direct empirical case against consensus (2.4's 32.3pp oracle gap).\n\n**5.2 — Per-boundary verification: 42.2pp reduction in error survival vs 2.3pp for end-checking.** See 4.3, [arXiv:2608.14588](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14588). The strongest single number in this report for verifier placement.\n\n**5.3 — Adding verification to a MAS: ChatDev ProgramDev 25.0% → 40.6% (+15.6pp).** See §1, [arXiv:2503.13657](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657).\n\n**5.4 — Trained critic models beat human reviewers on code.** Model-written critiques preferred over human critiques in **63%** of cases on code containing naturally occurring LLM errors; critics found hundreds of errors in ChatGPT training data rated *\"flawless\"*. Critical caveat, verbatim: *\"Critics can have limitations of their own, including hallucinated bugs that could mislead humans... but human-machine teams of critics and contractors catch similar numbers of bugs to LLM critics while hallucinating less than LLMs alone.\"* McAleese et al. (OpenAI), **\"LLM Critics Help Catch LLM Bugs\"**, [arXiv:2407.00215](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.00215), 2024. → A critic that can flag is worth more than a critic that can rewrite; hallucinated defects are the failure mode to budget for.\n\n**5.5 — Architectures without centralized verification propagate errors more.** Verbatim from [arXiv:2512.08296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296); quantified as 17.2× (independent) vs 4.4× (centralized) error amplification.\n\n### Verification FAILS — self-verification is unreliable\n\n**5.6 — Intrinsic self-correction DEGRADES accuracy across the board.** GPT-3.5: GSM8K 75.9% → 75.1% → 74.7%; CommonSenseQA **75.8% → 38.1%** → 41.8%; HotpotQA 26.0% → 25.0% → 25.0%. GPT-4 CommonSenseQA 82.0% → 79.5% → 80.0%. On GSM8K GPT-3.5 retains its initial answer 74.7% of the time and, among changes, is *\"more likely to modify a correct answer to an incorrect one\"* than the reverse. With ORACLE labels telling the model when it is wrong, self-correction works (GPT-3.5 GSM8K 75.9% → 84.3%; CommonSenseQA 75.8% → 89.7%). Huang et al., ICLR 2024, [arXiv:2310.01798](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798). → **The entire measured value of a review stage lives in the independence of its stop signal.** Same model, no external signal, review makes things worse. This is the strongest result in the report and it is directly load-bearing for protocol design.\n\n**5.7 — A critic stage can be net-negative even in a system built around it.** VERITAS ablation, Table 6, local models: removing the critic changed evidence accuracy 63.4% → 60.8% (−2.6pp) but IMPROVED combined verdict accuracy 71.4% → **73.2% (+1.8pp)**. Authors' note: *\"Critic removal has minimal impact ... indicating the iterative code-revision loop already provides sufficient error correction.\"* System-level numbers: 81.4% verdict accuracy with frontier models, 71.2% with 8–30B open-weight, 86.6% independently verifiable outputs. **VERITAS**, [arXiv:2604.12144](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12144), 2026. → If another loop already provides correction, a critic adds cost and can subtract accuracy. Ablate the critic; do not assume it.\n\n**5.8 — LLM judges favour their own generations, and the bias tracks self-recognition.** Verbatim: LLMs *\"score their own outputs higher than others' while human annotators consider them of equal quality\"*; GPT-4 and Llama 2 have *\"non-trivial accuracy at distinguishing themselves from other LLMs and humans\"*, and fine-tuning reveals *\"a linear correlation between self-recognition capability and the strength of self-preference bias\"* with the causal direction confirmed under controlled confounders. Panickssery, Bowman, Feng, **\"LLM Evaluators Recognize and Favor Their Own Generations\"**, NeurIPS 2024, [proceedings](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/7f1f0218e45f5414c79c0679633e47bc-Abstract-Conference.html) / [arXiv:2404.13076](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.13076). → A verifier that is the same model as the generator is a biased instrument, and the bias is causal, not incidental. Use a different model family for the verifier where you can.\n\n**5.9 — Sycophantic conformity in peer review among agents: modal-answer adoption up to 85.5%, correct-answer destabilisation up to 70.0%.** [arXiv:2605.00914](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00914). → Peer review among identical agents converges on the majority, not the truth. This is the mechanism behind 5.6 at the system level.\n\n---\n\n## What the evidence converges on for a divide-and-conquer protocol\n\nTwo angles agree on each of these, from independent papers:\n\n1. **Verify at every handoff, not at the end.** 42.2pp vs 2.3pp (4.3); confirmed by \"architectures without centralized verification propagate errors more\" and 17.2× vs 4.4× amplification (2.9/5.5).\n2. **The verifier's stop signal must be external to the generator.** Self-correction degrades (5.6); self-preference bias is causal (5.8); oracle labels recover the entire gain (5.6).\n3. **Select with a verifier; never aggregate by consensus vote.** PRM 78.2% vs majority 69.6% (5.1); consensus discarded already-correct answers in up to 32.3pp of cases (2.4).\n4. **Do not split tool-heavy sequential work, and do not split when the single-agent baseline is already strong.** −50.4% to −70.0% on PlanCraft, ~45% threshold (2.9); +0.008 to +0.096 single-agent advantage at matched budget on multi-hop (2.2); Anthropic excludes coding (2.10).\n5. **Hand a child a complete spec once, not incrementally.** 39% multi-turn drop and no recovery from a wrong turn (4.1); drift in 76–89% of generative tasks with \"lack of clarity\" at 25% of root causes (4.2); fail-to-ask-for-clarification at 6.8% of all MAST failures (§1).\n6. **Cost is superlinear in agent count; accuracy is not.** 36–49× tokens from 1→8 agents (3.1); 15× tokens for +90.2% unmatched (2.10); inverse-U in call count (3.5).\n7. **Every measured multi-agent win that survives matched compute is on parallel breadth-search work.** Finance +80.9%, BrowseComp +9.2% (2.9); mixture-of-agents +2.7pp only above the self-consistency plateau (2.11).\n\n---\n\n# SEARCHES RUN\n\n**Main loop — WebSearch, in order:**\n1. `MAST Multi-Agent System Failure Taxonomy arXiv 2503.13657`\n2. `Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail taxonomy failure modes percentages`\n3. `Anthropic engineering how we built our multi-agent research system token usage 15x`\n4. `multi-agent debate token cost scaling agent count diminishing returns measured arXiv`\n5. `LLMs Get Lost in Multi-Turn Conversation 39% performance drop arXiv 2505.06120`\n6. `error propagation compounding failure rate multi-agent LLM pipeline measured per-step arXiv`\n7. `LLM evaluators recognize and favor their own generations self-preference bias measured`\n8. `\"independent verifier\" OR \"critic agent\" multi-agent pipeline measured accuracy improvement ablation arXiv 2026`\n9. `multi-agent LLM system latency measured wall-clock scaling number of agents orchestration overhead benchmark`\n10. `measured information loss context handoff between LLM agents summarization compression degradation multi-agent arXiv`\n\n**Main loop — WebFetch, in order:** `arxiv.org/abs/2605.14102` · `arxiv.org/abs/2605.01566`\n\n---\n\n# GAPS\n\n**Version conflicts I could not resolve — treat the MAST percentages as approximate.**\n- MAST category splits differ by arXiv version: v2 gives FC1 41.77% / FC2 36.94% / FC3 21.30%; v3 gives 43.8% / 32.35% / 23.5%. Individual modes move too (step repetition 17.14% → 15.7%). The dataset grew from ~150 to 1,642 traces between versions. **Anyone quoting \"42/37/21\" is quoting v2.** I could not open the PDF to arbitrate — arXiv served an HTML error page to curl and the WebFetch PDF extract was unreadable binary.\n- MAST case-study numbers also differ by version: ChatDev ProgramDev baseline 25.0% (v1) vs 33.33% (v3); AG2 intervention +9.4% on MathChat (v2/v3) vs +5.0pp on GSM-Plus (v1).\n- [arXiv:2512.08296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296) differs by version: 180 vs 260 configurations, R² 0.513 vs 0.373, Finance +80.9% vs +80.8%. The ~45% single-agent threshold and the 17.2×/4.4× error-amplification figures come from v1 only and I did not re-confirm them in the current version.\n\n**Numbers I could not source.**\n- **Per-framework failure rates for MAST's 7 frameworks.** Only the range (\"41% to 86.7%\") and two point values (ChatDev ProgramDev 25.0%/33.33%, HumanEval 89.6%) are sourced. The individual rate for MetaGPT, HyperAgent, AppWorld, AG2, Magentic-One, OpenManus is **unverifiable** from what I retrieved — the paper presents them in Figure 1, which the HTML extract did not render as text.\n- **A study measuring spec degradation across an explicit agent-to-agent relay chain** — the \"telephone game\" experiment, where the same spec is passed N hops and fidelity is measured at each hop. I found the adjacent evidence (4.1 multi-turn, 4.2 drift, 4.3 per-boundary escape, 4.6 compression) but **no paper that measures spec fidelity as a function of relay depth directly.** This is the cleanest gap in the report and the closest thing to a direct answer for question 4.\n- **MAFBench ([arXiv:2602.03128](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03128)) supporting detail — unverifiable.** The 100× latency, 30% planning accuracy and 90%→30% coordination numbers are abstract-level. Framework count, benchmark count, model count and cost figures were not in what I retrieved.\n- **[arXiv:2502.08788](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08788) per-benchmark table — unverifiable.** Four fetch routes returned only abstract and references. The abstract claim is quotable; the per-benchmark accuracies are not. The paper appears to have been retitled \"If Multi-Agent Debate is the Answer, What is the Question?\".\n- **[arXiv:2403.02419](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.02419) inverse-U magnitudes — unverifiable.** Shown in figures, not tabulated; I cannot say where it peaks or how far it falls.\n- **No independent replication of Anthropic's 90.2%.** Internal eval, dataset not public, and no matched-token-budget arm — the single-agent baseline never got the 15× budget.\n- **No OpenAI or Microsoft official engineering blog with a measured multi-agent vs single-agent number was found.** Google is covered via [arXiv:2512.08296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.08296) (Google Research / DeepMind co-authored); Microsoft via [arXiv:2505.06120](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06120) and [arXiv:2510.00615](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.00615).\n- **AutoGen ([arXiv:2308.08155](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.08155)), ChatDev (2307.07924), AgentVerse (2308.10848) primary papers not fetched.** Only third-party measurements of ChatDev are sourced here.\n- **A search-result snippet claimed VERITAS showed −10.2pp / −4.9pp from broadening critic scope. That is NOT in the paper.** Table 6 measures critic REMOVAL, not extension, and gives −2.6pp / +1.8pp. Do not use the −10.2/−4.9 figures. Similarly, a first pass on ChromaFlow ([arXiv:2605.14102](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14102)) produced fabricated figures; only 54.72% / 50.94% / 56.60% / 12-of-20 / 11-of-20 are real, on n=53, so treat 2.x uses of it as directional only.\n- **Two on-topic 2026 leads not chased**, bounded by scope: [arXiv:2606.25514](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25514) (adaptive multi-agent scaffolding for issue resolution) and [arXiv:2605.23950](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23950) (\"Stop Comparing LLM Agents Without Disclosing the Harness\").\n\nSources: [arXiv:2503.13657](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.13657) ·
